**Centring is the one place a subtraction meets vectors nobody normalised, and it fails silently on the safest-looking configuration.** All 31 vector comparisons in this repo use `<=>`, which is cosine and therefore magnitude-invariant, so a non-unit stored vector has never cost any read path anything and no reader has ever had to care. `refreshCenteredEmbeddings` is the exception, and it is easy to miss because it does not look like one: it is a **subtraction**, and it fails twice over on rows of differing norm. `AVG` weights each row by its magnitude, so a long vector drags the library mean toward itself; and subtracting a fixed mean from rows of differing magnitude moves each by a different *proportion of itself*, so the results differ in **direction**, not merely in scale. Cosine cannot recover from that — the centred column becomes a confident set of wrong angles, and since [F-036](#f-036) makes centring worth **ndcg@100 20.5% → 31.9%**, corrupting it is expensive in exactly the place the gain came from. `storeEmbeddings` normalises nothing; it stores what the provider returned. Four things. (1) **It is currently inert, and measurably so** — norms over `embeddings_3072` on the live instance span **0.999930 to 1.000079 around an average of exactly 1.000000**, which is halfvec rounding of a vector that was unit-length when written. `gemini-embedding-001` at its native 3072 returns unit vectors, so `l2_normalize` here is the identity far below halfvec precision. **That inertness is the trap**: the call looks like dead code, and the configuration that proves it harmless is the one configuration where it does nothing. Do not delete it. (2) **It is a latent defect on the current schema, not a new requirement.** Seven of the eight `VALID_EMBEDDING_DIMENSIONS` are MRL truncations, where the norm depends on how much of that text's energy landed in the kept dimensions — Google's own documentation requires manual normalisation below 3072 — so an operator can already select a broken configuration today. Other providers return non-unit vectors at *every* dimension: Qwen3-Embedding's reference usage applies `F.normalize` after pooling and leaves it to the caller. (3) **This is the same argument `buildWeightedAverageEmbedding` already makes** for unit-normalising each item before summing ([F-037](#f-037)). That was the first place vectors are added rather than compared; this was the second, and it was missed for the same reason — the read paths are all cosine, so nothing downstream complains. Any future code that **sums or subtracts** these vectors is the third case and needs the same treatment; comparing them does not. (4) **Normalising here rather than in `storeEmbeddings` is deliberate.** This job is idempotent and re-run on demand, so the fix repairs existing rows with no re-embed; writing normalised vectors instead would leave old and new rows in different states until a full pass, and would discard what the provider actually returned — the only ground truth available when a model is behaving oddly. Still one statement, so F-036's all-or-nothing property is untouched. `l2_normalize` needs pgvector 0.7.0, which this schema already requires (`0024` and `0078` use halfvec, `0091` uses `binary_quantize`).

## F-091

**Centring was a manual job nothing chained, so every routine sync quietly armed a refusal.**

**Centring was a manual job nothing chained, so every routine sync quietly armed a refusal.** `refresh-embedding-centering` was `scheduleType: 'manual'` and `refreshCenteredEmbeddings` had exactly one caller — its own executor case. Meanwhile `generate-movie-embeddings` and `generate-series-embeddings` run on a **six-hour interval**. So the routine path was: new film syncs → gets embedded → lands with `embedding` filled and `embedding_centered` **NULL** → `isCenteringReady` flips false → and every viewer on a centred profile hits [F-036](#f-036)'s refusal row and finalizes `failed` with *"Taste profile space does not match available embeddings"*. Nothing was broken, nothing was misconfigured, and no amount of reading the recommender would find it — the trigger was a film arriving. The saving grace is the one already designed in: readers resolve the newest `status='completed'` run, so a refusal leaves the previous good run live and the page does not blank ([F-046](#f-046)). It degrades into staleness rather than absence, which is exactly why it could sit there unnoticed. Both jobs now call `centreAfterGeneration` before `completeJob`. Four things. (1) **Inside the job, not after it in the executor** — both embedding functions own their own progress record and call `completeJob` themselves, so centring afterwards would run against a job already reported finished, outside both the progress bar and the recorded duration. Each job gained a step for it (the series count is `shouldEmbedEpisodes ? 5 : 4`, since its whole bar already depends on episode scope). (2) **It is gated, and the gate is not gold-plating.** Rewriting the column is a full-table UPDATE — ~77 MB for 12,589 movies at 3072 halfvec — against jobs that fire four times a day and usually find nothing, so unconditional would mean rewriting the table to byte-identical values for the autovacuum churn and WAL volume of a real change. `centeringNeeded(generated, ready)` is pure, exported and pinned: **new vectors** (which land NULL *and* move the mean the rest of the column was centred against), or **an unready column**, which repairs an interrupted refresh — and is what makes a failed centring self-healing, since the next scheduled pass simply sees it unready and retries. (3) **It never rethrows.** The embeddings are written and good by that point, so failing the job would report the wrong thing; the error is logged loudly and the `!ready` condition collects it next pass. (4) **One copy, in `centering.ts`.** The movie and series pipelines are mirrored by design, but this is error handling rather than pipeline logic, and a drifting copy fails silently — one media type stops being servable for centred profiles while the other keeps working. Episodes have no centred column (`0154` covers the movie and series families only), so the series job passes `seriesGenerated` and centres series alone. `refresh-embedding-centering` stays for a manual sweep. Still unmodelled, and second-order rather than first: `embedding_space` records that a profile is centred, not *which mean* it was centred against, so re-centring after new arrivals shifts every item vector slightly while stored profiles reflect the old mean. Fifty new films against 12,589 move it very little and everything moves together, so relative geometry barely changes and the 30-day profile refresh sweeps it up — but it is real drift, and the space tag guarantees less than it appears to.

**A third condition, added after the fact: rows can LEAVE.** Every embedding table declares `REFERENCES movies(id) ON DELETE CASCADE` (`0007`, `0030`, `0078`), so a title removed from the media server takes its vector with it on the next sync — leaving **no NULL behind**. `generated` is 0 and `isCenteringReady` is true, so both original conditions said no work, while the mean the entire column is centred against still counted the departed films. It is the one case where the column looks complete and is wrong, and it cannot announce itself: the symptom is a silently stale origin, not a refusal. `centeringNeeded` now also compares the live row count against the count recorded at the last successful centring (`system_settings.embedding_centering_state`, keyed by media type **and model** — a set switch is not drift, and comparing one set's count against another's would fire a full rewrite for nothing). Three details. The count is written inside `refreshCenteredEmbeddings`, not at the gate, because the manual job calls that function directly and bookkeeping attached to the gate would leave a manual run writing a correct column and a stale count — after which the next embedding pass redoes the whole rewrite. An **unknown** previous count reads as needing work: that costs one rewrite on an instance upgrading into the check, against trusting a column whose history cannot be seen, which is the same asymmetry as everywhere else here. And the gate takes an **object** rather than four positionals — the two counts are both numbers, transposing them would still typecheck, and changing the shape makes every call site a compile error. Deliberately no threshold on the size of the drift: a fraction that "matters" is a number nobody has measured, and the check is a `COUNT` guarding a rewrite it now only triggers when something really changed.

**What the manual job is for, now that nothing needs it routinely.** Its description told operators to *"run it after any re-embed"* — true before `centreAfterGeneration` existed, and since then an instruction to perform a redundant 77 MB rewrite. The gate now covers new vectors, a half-filled column, and departed rows. What it cannot cover is a column that is **populated but wrong**: a bad mean from a buggy run reads as ready and leaves no NULL to repair, so only a deliberate re-run fixes it. That, plus forcing the work now rather than at the next embedding pass, is the job's remaining purpose and what its description now says. **Scheduling it would be wrong on both counts** — it is a full-table rewrite on a timer, which is precisely what the gate exists to avoid, and every centring shifts the space under stored profiles as described above, so a cadence buys strictly more of that drift in exchange for no signal at all.

## F-092

**The harness could only ever measure the model already in use, and read its neighbours off the one seed population where every model agrees.**

**The harness could only ever measure the model already in use, and read its neighbours off the one seed population where every model agrees.** Two independent gaps, both of which made `evaluate-recommender` unable to answer the question it exists for. (1) **`loadLibraryMatrix` resolved the model and the table for itself**, so comparing two models meant flipping the active configuration between runs -- which also flips which set `refreshCenteredEmbeddings` writes, so the two things you most want held still both moved at once. It now takes an optional `{ modelId, dimensions }`. Both fields are required because **the dimension names the table**: rows are keyed `(item, model)` inside a per-dimension table, so a model change leaves the previous set intact ([F-088](#f-088)) -- the storage property that makes an A/B possible without a re-embed, which nothing could reach. `runEvaluation` still measures one set and now reports which; the executor enumerates every set with rows, so an operator runs the job unchanged and gets a table per model. The matrix carries its own `modelId` for the same reason it already carries `centered`: two sets are indistinguishable by inspection, and a report that cannot name what it measured is not a comparison. **The confound to check before reading anything is `poolSize`** -- `loadLibraryMatrix` deliberately applies no library filter so the pool is stable, but a set the embedding job never finished is genuinely smaller, and both `medianPercentile` and a deep-cutoff ndcg read BETTER on a smaller pool, so a half-embedded model wins. The executor warns when sets disagree. `canonical_text` is stored per row, so the sibling confound is checkable rather than avoidable: join the two sets and assert the texts are identical, or you are comparing texts and not models. (2) **Seeds were unreachable.** `seedTitles` existed on `EvaluationOptions` and the executor never passed it, so the dump always ran on `popularSeedIds` -- the same unreachable-parameter shape as the deleted `getValidJobNames` ([F-076](#f-076)), sitting on the instrument this module's own header calls PRIMARY. It is not a cosmetic default: popularity selects canonical, heavily-written-about films, which is precisely where two embedding models agree, while the seeds that discriminate are the obscure, the stylistic and the non-English -- all three structurally excluded. Seeds now come from `system_settings.evaluation_seed_titles`, newline-separated because a film title may contain a comma. Three details. `resolveSeedIds` wraps both title columns in `unaccent()` (`0134`), because a non-English title is one of the seeds worth stressing a model on and nobody types "Ascenseur pour l'échafaud" with the accent -- STABLE, so it costs the trigram index, irrelevant for a handful of seeds once per run. A seed that resolves to a real film but has **no vector in this set** is now logged rather than silently filtered, because that is exactly the case that matters when two sets are compared: a seed present in one dump and absent from the other reads as the models disagreeing. And zero usable seeds logs why instead of returning quietly, which would delete the primary instrument from the report and look like it merely had nothing to say.

## F-093

**Two modes of one model collide on the set identity, and the second pass silently destroys the first.**

Embedding rows are keyed `UNIQUE(<item>_id, model)` inside a per-dimension table and every read filters `WHERE model = $n`, so that one string is the whole boundary between one population of vectors and another. It was built inline as `${provider}:${model}` in **nineteen places across five files** (`ai-provider.ts`, both `recommender/*/embeddings.ts`, `settings/handlers/aiConfig.ts`, `settings/handlers/recommendations.ts`) — the duplicated-predicate shape this repo keeps paying for, and here it was load-bearing rather than merely untidy.

**The failure it would have caused.** Once [F-038](#f-038) established that `input_type: semantic_similarity` is a real, distinct space, the obvious next move is to embed the library in it and compare against the existing default-mode set. With the identity built from provider and model alone, both are `openrouter:google/gemini-embedding-001`, both land in `embeddings_3072`, and the second pass `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`s straight over the first. The A/B destroys its own control, the sets panel shows one row where it should show two, and `getEmbeddingSetsReport` cannot see that anything happened. Nothing errors. Note `google/gemini-embedding-2` does **not** collide — its model id differs — which is exactly the kind of asymmetry that makes this look like it works right up until it does not.

**The fix is one pure module**, `lib/embeddingIdentity.ts`, pinned by `embeddingIdentity.test.ts`. Seven things matter.

1. **The absent case must be byte-identical to the legacy string.** Every row already on disk was written as `provider:model`. Any decoration at all — a trailing separator, a `~none`, a normalised case — makes the entire existing library invisible to the staleness join and to the recommender's `WHERE model = $n`, presenting as a library that lost its embeddings overnight. Three separate tests cover this: undefined, explicitly-undefined, and blank/whitespace (a cleared settings field arrives as `''`, not `undefined`).

2. **The separator is `~`, not `#` or `:`.** `:` and `/` are both already *inside* model ids (`google/gemini-embedding-001`; OpenRouter's `:free` and `:extended` variants), so neither can delimit. `#` fails differently and worse: the set id is a URL path segment in `DELETE /api/settings/ai/embeddings/sets/:model`, so the one caller that forgets `encodeURIComponent` has everything after the `#` silently dropped — quietly addressing the semantic set's id to the default set and deleting the wrong vectors. `~` is unreserved in RFC 3986 and survives a raw path intact.

3. **An unrecognised mode is dropped, never forwarded.** OpenRouter's `input_type` is an unconstrained string, so a typo would be accepted, ignored by the provider, and then stored in an identity claiming a mode the vectors were never embedded in. Dropping falls back to the default space, which is where everything already lives — the asymmetric-failure choice.

4. **A provider that cannot carry a mode may not store one.** Only `openrouter` (via `extraBody`) and `google` (via `providerOptions.taskType`) can send one. The settings route **rejects** the combination with a 400 rather than storing it, and `getEmbeddingInvocation` warns and drops if it meets one anyway.

5. **The model and the identity are handed out together.** `getEmbeddingModelInstance` is **replaced** by `getEmbeddingInvocation`, returning `{ model, providerOptions, setId, inputType }`. Not a wrapper — replaced, so every call site is a compile error until it is looked at. That is how the two API call sites outside `packages/core` were found (`assistant/handlers/chat.ts`, `settings/handlers/tasteProfile.ts`), neither of which appeared in a grep scoped to core. `embedMovies` previously fetched the model while `storeEmbeddings` separately re-read the config to build the identity — two `getFunctionConfig` calls, so an admin saving the AI card mid-pass could file half a batch under an identity naming a space it was not embedded in.

6. **The id travels on the row, not alongside the batch.** `EmbeddingResult.setId` is stamped where the vector is produced, and the three `storeEmbeddings` writers pass `embeddings.map(e => e.setId)` rather than `Array(n).fill(modelName)`. There is no second place for it to be decided.

7. **The assistant's `ToolContext` collapsed from two fields to one.** It carried `embeddingModel` *and* `embeddingModelId`, resolved by two separate calls, and three tools embed a query then filter on the id. A query embedded through one configuration and filtered against another id still returns a confident ranking of the wrong population. Now one `embedding: EmbeddingInvocation`.

**Nothing parses a set id in the data path** — `embeddingSets.ts` treats it as opaque throughout, which is why a suffix could be added without touching it. `describeEmbeddingSetId` exists for display only, and the web panel mirrors it by hand (web-never-imports-core); a drift there costs a label, never a query.

**One door to the embedding SDK, because a prefix transforms the input rather than the request.** A mode delivered as a parameter is either sent or not; a mode delivered as a *text prefix* means the caller has to change what it embeds, and a call site that embeds raw text produces a vector with the right width, a unit norm and plausible neighbours, sitting in a different space from every row beside it. Nothing downstream can detect that, and TypeScript will not help — an unused `prepareText` is not an error.

The count is the argument. There were **eleven** `embed`/`embedMany` call sites across core and the API: both library passes, episodes, `embedText`, the similarity graph's query, three assistant tools, two settings handlers and the global search route. `routes/search/index.ts` was **missed twice** while enumerating them by hand, in a session specifically counting them. A convention would not have survived that.

So `getEmbeddingInvocation` returns `embedOne` / `embedBatch`, which apply the prefix internally, and `embeddingCallSites.test.ts` scans `packages/core/src` and `apps/api/src` for any direct SDK call outside `ai-provider.ts`. Two properties make that test worth having rather than decorative: it asserts the walk actually reached the tree (a silently-empty scan would pass forever, including a check that it reached the file that was missed by hand), and it was **verified to fail** by injecting a violation and confirming it reported the right file and line before being removed.

`testProviderConnection` is inside the gateway file and legitimately embeds the literal `'test'` unconditioned — it is checking reachability, not producing a stored vector.

**Two defects surfaced while enumerating those sites, both in `routes/search/index.ts`.** Its `getQueryEmbedding` built its own OpenAI client from `getEmbeddingModel()` — the *legacy* bare model name in `system_settings.embedding_model` — plus `getOpenAIApiKey()`. On any instance not using OpenAI that returns null, so semantic search was silently off and the route quietly degraded to text and trigram matching; on an instance that did have an OpenAI key while embedding its library with something else, it would have scored OpenAI query vectors against Gemini item vectors. And its ANN join carried **no `WHERE e.model` filter** — harmless while a dimension table holds one set, actively wrong the moment a second lands beside it, which is exactly what the set-identity work above enables. `similarity/index.ts` filters on `model` in all eight of its queries; this route was the only outlier.

**Cost of switching the mode**, which the settings card states in an inline warning: it starts a new set beside the old one, so the library keeps answering from the current space until embeddings → centring → **`rebuild-taste-profiles`** → recommendations have all run. Moving item vectors does not move a stored centroid, and `isProfileStale` will not notice for up to 30 days (F-088).

## F-094

**The evaluation's report is longer than the log that carries it, and the half that gets thrown away is the comparison.**

`evaluate-recommender` writes its entire report through `addLog`, and the job log is trimmed twice on the way out: the live buffer to `LIVE_LOG_LIMIT` 500 on every append past the limit, the stored copy to `STORED_LOG_LIMIT` 300 when the run is filed. `condenseLogs` keeps a 30-entry head and as much tail as the budget allows ([F-082](#f-082)).

**The report does not fit.** Measured on a real run: 14 seeds × 2 variants × 14 lines is 392 entries of neighbour dump alone, plus the header, the two summary tables, the by-history block and one row per qualified viewer — roughly **450 entries per embedding set**. The executor measures every set with rows ([F-092](#f-092)), which is the only sound way to compare two, so a two-set run emits ~900 entries into a 300-entry window.

**What the operator was handed.** A 40-minute run over two sets of the same library — `openrouter:google/gemini-embedding-001` and `…~semantic_similarity@google-vertex`, both 12,589 titles, both fully centred. What survived was set 1's summary table at the top, 201 entries elided, and a neighbour dump at the bottom. The dump belongs to whichever set ran **last**, and nothing on the page said so: `logNeighbours` printed its section heading with no set id on it. The result is worse than a truncated report, because it reads as a coherent one — a table and a dump that appear to describe the same thing and do not. The first four seeds (*Chime*, *Argentina, 1985*, *The Insult*, *The Teachers' Lounge*) were also gone, and those are the obscure non-English ones chosen precisely because they discriminate.

**The elision marker was lying, which is what made the loss unreadable.** `condenseLogs` is called on every append once over budget, and each call recomputed `elided` from the list in front of it — a list already condensed, whose marker it then dropped and replaced. In steady state that is one new entry per call, so the live console of a job that had discarded thousands of lines reported **"2 earlier entries not kept"**. The 201 in the stored log is not a cumulative figure either: it is exactly `500 − 30 head − 269 tail`, i.e. a description of the buffer's size, not of the run's loss. Reading it as cumulative led to the confident and wrong conclusion that only one set had been measured. A marker now carries its own count in `data` and a later pass adds to it instead of starting over; detection is on that field and **not on the message text**, which is prose and could be reworded or translated.

**Three changes, and the ordering of the first two is the whole point.**

1. **The set id is on the neighbour heading**, not only on the summary table 400 entries above it.
2. **Every set's figures are re-emitted after the loop**, as one table with the set numbered on each row. The tail is the half that survives, so that is where the comparison has to live. This costs six lines and is the only reason a two-set run is readable at all.
3. **The figures are archived** (`0156`, `evaluation_runs` / `evaluation_metrics` / `evaluation_neighbours`), because a log window that fits today stops fitting the moment someone adds a seed.

**Why a table and not a file.** A file inside the container answers the durability question and then disappears on the next recreate — which is exactly when someone deploys the change they were measuring. Rows also make the thing the operator actually wants (several runs, several models, several seed lists, merged into one sheet) a query rather than a concatenation, which is why every exported row carries `model`, `pool_size` and `run_at` rather than leaving provenance in a second sheet. Nothing prunes them: a run is ~450 rows across both detail tables, so a hundred runs is 45k rows, and accumulating across models is the entire point.

**The archive write is swallowed.** Forty minutes of measurement has already happened by then; failing the job over the bookkeeping would throw away the log report as well. Same rule as `pruneOldRecommendationRuns` ([F-047](#f-047)).

**Two details decide whether the CSV is readable, both in `evaluation/csv.ts`** — database-free so it can be pinned, since this is the shape that otherwise gets copied into each export route and drifts.

- **A UTF-8 BOM.** Excel on Windows reads a BOM-less UTF-8 file in the system codepage, and this library is full of accented titles (*Utøya: July 22*, *Aniara*, *Alice In The Cities*). Without it the export opens as mojibake and reads as a data bug rather than an encoding one.
- **A formula guard on text cells only, with a numeric-text exclusion that is load-bearing.** Excel and LibreOffice execute a cell opening with `=`, `+`, `-` or `@`. But **pg returns NUMERIC as TEXT** (the invariant, running backwards for once), so every score, share and cosine in these exports reaches the escaper as a *string* — and a negative one opens with a minus. Guarding it would prefix an apostrophe and turn the entire cosine column into text, breaking exactly the pivot the file exists for. So a string matching a plain number is passed through untouched, while `-A1` is still defused. Pinned by `csv.test.ts`.

## F-095

**The retrieval mode is one lever with two opposite effects, and the metrics cannot see either of them.**

Five embedding sets were measured on one library, all at **12,589 rows fully centred**, so `poolSize` — the confound [F-092](#f-092) says to check first — is eliminated by construction: `openrouter:google/gemini-embedding-001`, the same pinned `~semantic_similarity@google-vertex`, `gemini-embedding-2~semantic_similarity`, bare `gemini-embedding-2`, and `qwen/qwen3-embedding-8b` at 4096. Macro over 33 qualified viewers, holdout 20, 18 seeds.

**The metrics rank them and the ranking does not decide anything.** Centred, overall: gemini-2~semantic 89.40% median / 12.83% ndcg@20 / 26.67% ndcg@100; bare gemini-2 89.42 / 11.88 / 26.76; 001 87.54 / 9.30 / 23.79; qwen 84.82 / 7.90 / 23.36; 001~semantic 84.83 / 9.85 / 20.28. Baselines sane (random 50.96% median, 0.46% ndcg@100; rating-only 68.92 / 8.51), so the harness is measuring something. But the two gemini-2 configurations are a **dead heat** — the prefixed one wins shallow (ndcg@20, recall@100), the bare one wins deep (ndcg@500 51.32 vs 49.21, recall@500 40.72 vs 38.68), and the medians differ by 0.02pp. Nothing in `metrics.ts` separates them, which is what [F-057](#f-057) means by calling it a guard rail rather than a target.

**Centring replicates on four spaces it was never derived from.** Raw→centred ndcg@100: 001 +41%, bare gemini-2 +43%, gemini-2~semantic +50%, qwen +64%, 001~semantic +65% relative. It is the largest single effect in the dataset, larger than any difference between models. Contrary to a widely repeated claim, unit-norm MRL embeddings do **not** make it unnecessary — normalising fixes magnitude, centring removes a shared *direction*, and every canonical text here is built from one template, so a large common component is exactly what to expect.

**The aggregate ndcg@20 win exists in a minority of viewers.** Paired per viewer, gemini-2~semantic minus 001: median percentile +1.86pp (t=2.36, **22/33** improved), ndcg@100 +2.87pp (t=1.48, 19/33), ndcg@20 +3.54pp (t=1.97, **13/33**). The headline number — a 38% relative gain on the cutoff users actually see — is the one where *most viewers get worse*, and the positive mean comes from a few winners at +28pp against many small losers. Median percentile is the only metric whose win is also a per-viewer majority, and it is the smallest. Same trap as the decade gradient: read the aggregate as a mechanism and it describes nobody.

**`input_type` on 001 is a measurable loss, which contradicts the a priori argument in [F-038](#f-038).** That entry reasons that this library's task is symmetric — item↔item, and a taste centroid is a mean of item vectors — so `semantic_similarity` is the mode it wants. Measured, on the identical model and pool, the pinned semantic set ranks **last of five** while the unmoded set ranks second. The pin is not the cause: both upstreams agree on the unmoded vector, which is the same fact that makes a pin-without-mode safe. The symmetry argument is sound about the *task* and simply does not predict what the mode does to the *space*.

**On gemini-2 the prefix does two things at once, and they pull in opposite directions.** Centred, over 18 seeds — mean same-country share of the top ten, and the share of top-ten slots holding a neighbour that shares a title word with the seed:

| | country share | title-word share |
|---|---|---|
| gemini-2 `~semantic_similarity` | **75.0%** | 10.0% |
| gemini-2 bare | 83.9% | **3.3%** |
| gemini-001 | 87.2% | 2.8% |
| qwen3-8b | 75.6% | 6.7% |

Removing the prefix fixes the lexical attraction outright — to 001's level — and hands back most of the nationality gain. Bare is higher than prefixed on 13 of 18 seeds and tied on 4, so this is not an average concealing a mixed picture. The `task: sentence similarity` cue is a single mechanism producing both effects, and the documentation's own mode table contains the tell: it lists *near-duplicate detection* and *recommendations* as use cases for the same mode, and those are opposite requirements about whether surface overlap should count.

**The lexical attraction is inside the model, not in a ranking layer.** This app has no BM25, no term-frequency scoring and no keyword boost anywhere in the vector path; the neighbour dump is a bare pgvector `<=>` kNN. `scripts/probe-title-label.mjs` holds the seed, the pool and the metric constant and varies only the document text and the prefix, ranking a pool of known-wrong and known-right neighbours drawn from the archived dump. It reports **separation** — mean intruder rank minus mean anchor rank — which should be positive. On the shipping document with the prefix it is **−0.56**: the intruders are *closer* than the correct neighbours. On *The Conversation*, both *Conversation Piece*s took ranks 1 and 2 ahead of the entire 70s surveillance cluster.

**Four treatments, and three of the results are negative.**

| gemini-2 separation | title bare | labelled | title at end | title removed |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `task: sentence similarity` | **−0.56** | −0.56 | +0.54 | +2.43 |
| no prefix | +1.17 | +1.33 | +1.17 | +2.12 |
| `task: clustering` | +1.33 | +1.01 | +1.96 | +2.43 |
| *gemini-001, any prefix* | +2.27 | +2.27 | +2.27 | +2.27 |

**There is one ceiling and it is reachable four ways.** Removing the title, changing the prefix, or using 001 all land between +2.1 and +2.4, and nothing combines past it — prefix-off *plus* stripped (+2.12) is no better than either alone. Title tokens and task cue contribute somewhat independently and both are bounded by the same limit, which is what a real ceiling looks like rather than an additive pair of fixes.

**`task: clustering` is lexically clean but not measurably cleaner than sending nothing** (+1.33 vs +1.17, inside the half-rank noise floor of this pool). Its *Alice In The Cities* result is the cleanest single case measured anywhere — anchors 1–5, intruders 6–8 — but on the axis the probe can see it buys nothing over the bare set already shipped. Its whole remaining case is the untested one: whether it recovers `sentence similarity`'s nationality benefit without the lexical cost, which no probe can answer because same-country share needs kNN over the whole library and therefore a full embed pass.

- **Labelling the title does nothing.** 25 of 26 rankings identical and separation unchanged to two decimals. The model is not reading "first bare string" as a role it can be talked out of; it reads *Conversation* and *Evil* as content words wherever they sit and however they are tagged. This rules out the whole class of re-framing fixes.
- **Moving the title to the end is a half-measure** (+0.54 with the prefix, a literal no-op without it). Only deleting the tokens works.
- **gemini-001 is immune, not merely less affected**: +2.27 under all four treatments, identical to two decimals. The title field does not participate in its space at all, so this is not a flaw in the canonical text that every model suffers.
- **The two fixes are not additive.** Prefix-off + stripped (+2.12) is no better than prefix-on + stripped (+2.43), and both land where 001 already sits untreated. That is the ceiling, reachable three different ways.

**Country concentration overstates the quality gap, and the neighbour dumps say so.** The measure earns its place — 001's *Aniara* neighbours include *Midsommar*, *Triangle of Sadness* and *Stormskerry Maja*, Nordic films that are not science fiction, which is [F-084](#f-084)'s nationality detector caught firing. But bare gemini-2 scores 83.9% and returns *The Colony*, *Cargo*, *Stowaway*, *High Life*, *Space Sweepers*, *Slingshot*, *Europa Report* — concept-correct contained-spacecraft films that happen to share a passport. A high share is not by itself the failure; **read the dump before treating the number as one.**

**Decision: bare `gemini-embedding-2`, no mode.** Best deep retrieval of any set, lexical artifact gone, neighbours read well, and the vectors already existed — the switch costs only `rebuild-taste-profiles` and a regenerate, since moving item vectors does not move a stored centroid ([F-088](#f-088)). The prefixed-plus-stripped-title configuration has a higher ceiling on paper and is **not** worth the bill yet: the probe measured the lexical artifact only, nothing has measured what removing the title does to ndcg or to country concentration, and a `CANONICAL_TEXT_VERSION` bump marks all five sets stale while rebuilding only the active one — after which the evaluator will measure v3 and v4 documents side by side in one table with nothing saying so. The archived runs ([F-094](#f-094)) are what make that experiment answerable later, since `splitHoldout` is deterministic and the pool is unchanged, so a v4 set can be compared against its own v3 figures rather than against whatever the next multi-set run prints.

**None of this was visible from the job log**, and that is what the archive bought. The comparison spans two runs a day apart and five sets; every figure above came from the CSVs.

**What shipped from this, in the settings vocabulary.** `clustering` was added to `EMBEDDING_INPUT_TYPES` — it is a valid native `task_type` on 001 as well as a documented prefix on gemini-2, so it is testable on *both*, including on the model with the highest measured nationality clustering. `search_query` was **withheld from the dropdown but left in the vocabulary**: on 001 it is provably a no-op (that model's default space already is `RETRIEVAL_QUERY`) and on gemini-2 it is refused for want of a verified prefix, so offering it only invites a full re-embed into a new set id holding vectors that already exist. Deleting it outright would be [F-093](#f-093)'s orphaning failure for any instance that had selected it, which is why the removal is one list in the web bundle and not the union in core. The probe can carry the lexical half of the clustering question and no further: **same-country share needs kNN over the whole library, so only a full embed pass can answer whether clustering recovers `sentence similarity`'s nationality benefit** — which is now the single open question in this whole line of work.

**`task: clustering` was then measured end to end, and it closes the mode question.** Same library, same 12,589-row pool, same 33 viewers. It takes the best shallow metrics of anything tested — ndcg@20 **13.57%** and ndcg@100 **28.35%**, against 12.83/26.67 for `sentence similarity` and 11.88/26.76 for bare — while taking the *worst* median percentile of the three gemini-2 configurations (88.27% against 89.42) and the worst recall@500. But on the two measures the neighbour dump exists to provide it is **indistinguishable from bare**: same-country share **83.9%, identical to one decimal**, and title-word share 3.9% against 3.3%, a difference of one film in 180 slots. The metrics edge does not survive the per-viewer check either — clustering minus bare is +1.69pp on ndcg@20 with **9 of 33 viewers improved**, +1.59pp on ndcg@100 at 15/33, and **−1.14pp** on median percentile, with no t above 1.1. A best-in-class aggregate produced by a quarter of the population is the same artifact as the earlier ndcg@20 headline, caught by the same check.

**The four configurations order perfectly on one axis, and that is the finding.** Same-country share against title-word share: `sentence similarity` 75.0/10.0, `clustering` 83.9/3.9, bare 83.9/3.3, gemini-001 87.2/2.8. Monotone, with a mechanism — the more a space attends to short salient strings like the title, the less it attends to the enumerated metadata tail where director, studio and country live. Four points is suggestive rather than proof, but it means **the mode lever moves one knob, not two: there is no prefix that buys nationality spread without paying in title matching.** Stop looking for a fifth mode. `clustering` stays in the vocabulary because it is measured and harmless, not because it is worth switching to.

**It is also what makes the deferred title-strip experiment the only intervention that leaves the curve.** Every mode trades along it; removing the title from the canonical text removes the thing being traded, so a mode may attend to titles as hard as it likes and find nothing there. The probe already puts `sentence similarity` + stripped title at **+2.43** separation, level with untreated gemini-001, and if same-country share holds near 75% — plausible, since the title is not one of the nationality-coded fields — that configuration is strictly better than every row above. Unmeasured, and the only way to measure it is a `CANONICAL_TEXT_VERSION` bump and a re-embed, compared against archived run `c26135ae` rather than against whatever the next multi-set run prints.

### Addendum: a second model, and the first clean split between the two instruments

**`voyageai/voyage-4-large` at 1024 was embedded and measured against the shipping set, and it is the first configuration where the neighbour dump and the metrics point opposite ways.** Every comparison above varied a *mode* on one model family; this varies the model. Run `10c4faa3` (voyage) against run `fcffd617` (bare `gemini-embedding-2`), 46 minutes apart the same afternoon, both at the full 12,589-row centred pool, both macro over the same 33 qualified viewers with holdout 20 and 18 seeds.

**The pairing is provable, and the proof is free.** `random` and `rating` are byte-identical across the two runs on all seven metrics (0.5087 / 0.0000 / 0.0046 / 0.0973 and 0.6892 / 0.0462 / 0.0870 / 0.2352), and neither baseline reads an embedding — so the holdout split is the same one, and this is a genuine paired test rather than two passes at a moving target. Watch history moves between runs: the figures in the table above came from an earlier bare-gemini-2 run and differ from `fcffd617` by up to 0.3pp. **When pairing, use the contemporaneous run, not the archived headline.**

**Metrics, centred, overall:**

| | median | ndcg@20 | ndcg@100 | ndcg@500 | recall@20 | recall@100 | recall@500 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| gemini-2 bare @3072 | **89.35** | **11.87** | **26.48** | **51.02** | **5.81** | **15.79** | **40.75** |
| voyage-4-large @1024 | 86.24 | 9.90 | 23.54 | 42.47 | 4.50 | 13.63 | 31.46 |
| delta | −3.11 | −1.97 | −2.94 | **−8.55** | −1.31 | −2.16 | **−9.29** |

**The paired per-viewer check passes this time, and that is the whole difference from the earlier ndcg@20 headline.** Voyage minus gemini-2, over 33 viewers: median percentile −3.12pp (t=−2.26, voyage better for **7/33**), ndcg@500 −8.55pp (t=−3.47, **6/33**), recall@500 −9.29pp (t=−3.46, **5/33**). Compare the finding this file already records — gemini-2~semantic's +38% relative ndcg@20 over 001 improved 13 of 33 and was an artifact of a few large winners. Here the deep-cutoff advantage is a **majority effect with a real t**, and it is the direction the aggregate already claimed. An aggregate that survives the per-viewer check is worth acting on; that is what the check is for, not to veto everything.

**ndcg@20 is the weakest instrument in this harness and it is the cutoff people care about.** 14 of 33 viewers **tie at zero** on it — at a cutoff of 20 out of 12,589, most viewers score nothing under either model — so it resolves 19 viewers, t=−0.80, and cannot separate these two sets at all. Read @500 and treat @20 as decoration. The same caution applies to `recall_20` (14 ties, t=−0.97).

**Raw and centred disagree about where voyage loses, and that is informative.** Raw, the two are level at the top and voyage is *ahead*: ndcg@20 5.83 against 5.69 (+2.5% relative), recall@20 2.70 against 2.39 (+13%), with gemini-2 ahead only at depth (ndcg@500 38.37 against 34.86). Centred, gemini-2 leads on every one of the seven. **Centring replicates on a sixth space and the first non-Google one** — voyage ndcg@100 15.30 to 23.54, **+54%** relative, against gemini-2's +45%, both inside the established +41–65% band — but it is worth *more at the top* to gemini-2, which is what erases voyage's raw shallow edge. Voyage's near-field advantage is a property of its raw space that centring does not carry forward.

**Country concentration: the aggregate gap is three seeds, not a population.** 76.7% against 83.9% reads decisive. Per seed, voyage is lower on **9 of 18**, tied on 5 and *higher* on 4, and half the aggregate gap is *Alice In The Cities* alone (30% against 80%), with *The Teachers' Lounge* and *The Worst Person In The World* at −30pp each supplying most of the rest. Title-word share is 2.8% against 3.9% — **two slots in 180**, which is noise. **An earlier reading of this data claimed voyage escapes the country-versus-lexical curve those four gemini configurations trace; the paired numbers do not support that** and it should not be repeated. The measurement is comparable, at least: this tokeniser reproduces the archived bare-gemini-2 country share to the decimal (83.9%) and its title-word share to within one slot (3.9 against 3.3).

**The lexical artifact is in the shipping set too, and the probe understated where it sits.** This entry records both *Conversation Piece*s taking ranks 1 and 2 — that was `probe-title-label.mjs`, ranking a curated pool against the *prefixed* document. In the live centred kNN over the whole library on **bare** gemini-2 they are still there, at **ranks 3 and 4**, and the rest of that list drifts to era-and-nationality rather than surveillance: *Cisco Pike*, *The Gambler*, *The Long Goodbye*, *The Laughing Policeman*. Voyage returns neither *Conversation Piece* and gives *Blow Out* at 1, then *The Ear*, *Caché*, *The Parallax View*, *Rear Window*, *The Lives Of Others*. So **"removing the prefix fixes the lexical attraction outright" is too strong**: it demotes it two ranks in the index that ships.

**Read the dumps and the two models differ in kind, not in quality.** Voyage is concept-led — *The Teachers' Lounge* returns a school-and-teacher cluster spanning Poland, Germany, France, Russia, Cuba, Japan and the US; *The Conversation* returns surveillance across four countries. Bare gemini-2 is corpus-led — *Alice In The Cities* returns *The American Friend*, *The Goalie's Anxiety At The Penalty Kick*, *Wings Of Desire*, *Radio On* and *Stranger Than Paradise*, which is New German Cinema and its direct descendants, a defensible cluster rather than [F-084](#f-084)'s detector misfiring. Both find Joachim Trier for *The Worst Person In The World*; gemini-2's *Aniara* is tighter on register (*Cargo*, *Black Crab*, *Europa Report*) where voyage reaches for the bigger-budget space films. Neither dump embarrasses itself, which is why the dumps could not decide this one.

**Decision: bare `gemini-embedding-2` stays, on coverage.** One embedding set backs the recommender, the Explore graph, `/similar`, semantic search and the evidence carousel, and those want different things. The recommender is a centroid query over up to `maxCandidates` rows followed by MMR over a shortlist, so **deep recall is what it actually spends**; the top ten is what the graph and `/similar` spend. A −9.3pp recall@500 for 26 of 33 viewers outweighs a top-ten edge that the paired test cannot resolve. Splitting the two — voyage for neighbours, gemini-2 for the recommender — is not a setting: [F-088](#f-088)'s sets coexist in storage but exactly one is read, and serving two would be a feature with its own staleness and centring story.

**The switch also surfaced that `EVIDENCE_CAUSAL_MIN_COSINE` was never re-derived when the model changed.** Raw rank-1 / rank-10 cosine medians over the 18 seeds: gemini-2 **0.806 / 0.763**, voyage **0.633 / 0.570**, with voyage's rank-1 *maximum* at 0.726 across all 18. [F-051](#f-051) set 0.72 when the active model was 001 and library-wide rank 10 landed 0.68–0.72. On gemini-2 rank 10 now sits at 0.763, so the gate passes nearly everything and the causal heading has quietly stopped discriminating; on voyage almost nothing would clear it and *"Why We Think You'll Like This"* would never appear. The rule that the constant is raw-cosine-scoped to the embedding that produced it now has a worked case in **both** directions — too loose and too tight — rather than a warning. Caveat on the numbers: the evidence lookup is a kNN restricted to titles the viewer has watched, a different and smaller population than a library-wide top ten, so these medians indicate the band and are not themselves a re-derivation.

## F-096

**A supported embedding width is a constant AND a migration, and the constant alone breaks the panel that would show you it is broken.**

`VALID_EMBEDDING_DIMENSIONS` (core `lib/ai-provider.ts`) shipped eight widths — 256 through 4096 — and `perplexity/pplx-embed-v1-4b` is 2560, so it could not be selected at all: `getCurrentEmbeddingDimensions` reads a model's declared width and `getEmbeddingTableSuffix` turns it into a table name, throwing on anything outside the list before a single vector is requested. Adding 2560 to the list is the obvious half of the fix and the dangerous one on its own.

**The coupling is not "should", it is "or the page 500s".** `listStoredSets` (`lib/embeddingSets.ts`) builds one `UNION ALL` across `${base}_${d}` for **every** entry in the list with no `to_regclass` guard, so a width whose tables do not exist does not degrade the Embeddings panel — it fails every load of it with `relation "embeddings_2560" does not exist`. Two more unguarded readers sit beside it: `POST /api/settings/ai/embeddings/clear` `TRUNCATE`s all three families per width, and the episode-storage route `UNION ALL`s a `COUNT` across every width. Exactly one caller resolves existence first — `purge.ts`, via `getExistingTables`, and its own comment explains why (a missing relation inside a transaction aborts it, so every later statement fails 25P02 whatever the JS `catch` does). So the safe-looking half of the codebase is the minority, and the constant's doc comment now says the migration comes first.

**A width added now must arrive with the columns three later migrations added, or it is a dimension that quietly behaves unlike its neighbours.** `0157` mirrors `0078`'s base shape and indexes, plus `0138`'s `text_version`/`updated_at` and `0154`'s `embedding_centered` — the last two on the **movie and series families only**, because episode canonical text reads no enrichment column and has nothing to go stale against. Index is plain `halfvec_cosine_ops` HNSW as `0078` uses through 3072, **not** `0091`'s binary-quantised workaround: that exists because pgvector's HNSW tops out at 4000 dimensions and 4096 is over it, while 2560 is under and can answer an exact cosine rather than a Hamming approximation needing re-rank. Nothing was needed for the taste side — `0079` and `0086` already made every profile and interest column a dimension-free `halfvec`.

**OpenRouter's catalogue endpoint confirms a model exists and will not tell you how wide it is.** `GET /api/v1/models` returns 418 entries and **zero** embedding models — not even the three already shipped — so the public list is useless for this role; `GET /api/v1/models/:slug/endpoints` answers per slug and is how `perplexity/pplx-embed-v1-4b` was confirmed against two 404ing guesses. It gives modality (`text->embeddings`), 32K context, $0.03/M, and one upstream endpoint (Perplexity, int8) — that last fact meaning **no provider pin is needed**, since [F-038](#f-038)'s pin requirement exists only where two upstreams disagree. It does **not** publish the output width, and its `supported_parameters` is visibly boilerplate (`temperature`, `top_k`, `frequency_penalty` on an embedding model), so it is not evidence either way. The width came from Perplexity's own docs and the HF model card, agreeing on **2560 native, Matryoshka-truncatable to 128**, and has **since been measured directly** — one call with no `dimensions` returns 2560, so `0157`'s DDL is confirmed rather than trusted. Since the declared width picks the table, guessing it is how a library reads as permanently empty; measuring it costs a fraction of a cent and `scripts/probe-embedding-shape.mjs` is the instrument.

**The interesting find is in the model card, and it measured larger than the card implies: it returns "unnormalized int8-quantized embeddings" at an L2 norm of 15.90.** Every earlier argument in this repo about normalisation ([F-037](#f-037), [F-036](#f-036) rule 6) is framed as *inert at a native dimension, live only under MRL truncation*, with `gemini-embedding-001` at 3072 as the worked case. pplx breaks that framing: 2560 **is** its native width and the vectors still arrive non-unit, because the served endpoint is quantised. So the per-item `l2Normalize` in `buildWeightedAverageEmbedding` and the normalise-before-mean in `refreshCenteredEmbeddings` are load-bearing here from the first run rather than latent. Harmless for the 31 `<=>` comparisons, which are cosine and magnitude-invariant; decisive for the two places vectors are **summed**. The comments that stated this as a count ("seven of the eight entries") were both stale and misleading and now say the durable thing: for any one model at most one width is native, and a native width is not itself a promise.

**It also takes no mode, and that is a design statement rather than a gap.** The card says outright it is deliberately not instruction-tuned — "you can embed the text you want to index directly, without having to choose or maintain an instruction prefix" — so unlike `gemini-embedding-2`, whose mode turned out to live in a text prefix nobody had documented ([F-038](#f-038)), there is no hidden lever to go measuring for later. Catalogued with no `recommendedInputType`, like Qwen, and `embeddingCatalog.test.ts`'s requirement that every entry carry an `inputTypeNote` is what forces that to be said rather than left blank.

## F-097

**A default that is true of one model is a lie about every other, and here it cost a re-embed rather than an error.**

The Retrieval mode control appeared for **every** model on a mode-capable provider, offering Google's task taxonomy against Qwen and pplx-embed — whose own catalogue notes say they take no mode at all. Reported as confusing, and it was worse than that.

**Why it was not merely cosmetic.** `resolveInputTypeDelivery` defaulted an undeclared model's mechanism to `'parameter'`. OpenRouter's `input_type` is an unconstrained string, so selecting `semantic_similarity` on Qwen sent a field the model ignores and returned the unmoded vector — while `embeddingSetId` put `~semantic_similarity` in the identity regardless, because by [F-093](#f-093)'s hardest-won rule it reads the config and **nothing else**. The result is a full library re-embed, paid for, writing byte-identical vectors under a second name; the sets report then shows two sets that are one population, and an evaluation comparing them measures nothing. Nothing throws at any point. The visible end of the same fault was `requiresProviderPin` demanding a **Gemini** upstream (`google-vertex`) before it would save a **Qwen** model.

**The fix is that the catalogue must say what it can do, and absent must mean none.** `resolveInputTypeDelivery` no longer defaults; a model with no declared `inputTypeMechanism` resolves to `'none'`, which is the same branch a prefix-conditioned model asked for an unverified prefix already took — so the settings route's existing 400 ("no verified way to apply this mode") starts firing for the case it was written for, and `requiresProviderPin` goes quiet because a pin cannot be load-bearing for a mode that is never sent. This extends the existing rule that only *verified* prefixes ship: only verified *mechanisms* are offered.

**The default was protecting exactly one entry, and it was not one of the OpenRouter ones.** Native `google`'s `gemini-embedding-001` declared no mechanism and relied entirely on the default, because its `taskType` rides in `providerOptions` rather than the request body — so flipping the default without looking would have silently stopped native Google sending any mode, on the one provider where no routing ambiguity exists. It now declares `"inputTypeMechanism": "parameter"` explicitly. **The catalogue test that would have caught this did not, because it iterates OpenRouter's models only**; there is now one that sweeps every provider offering embeddings, plus a named regression test for the native Google entry.

**Deliberately not fixed at the identity.** The honest-looking repair is to have `embeddingSetId` drop a mode the model cannot deliver. That is precisely the second argument [F-093](#f-093) removed after it produced the worst failure in that file's history — the writer knew the mechanism, sixteen readers did not, and rows were written under one name and looked for under another. The identity stays a pure function of config; the guard belongs at the write boundary, where the settings route already sits.

**The UI keeps a stored mode visible even once the model stops declaring one.** Hiding the control outright would strand an existing setting where it can neither be seen nor cleared — the same failure `embeddingInputTypeOptions` avoids by re-adding a current value that is off the offered list ([F-095](#f-095) rule 6).

**Then it was measured, and the blanket claim under "`dimensions` is deliberately not plumbed" turns out to be model-specific.** `scripts/probe-embedding-shape.mjs` — one call with no `dimensions` for the native width and L2 norm, one with `dimensions: N` plus `provider: { require_parameters: true }` for MRL, every configuration sent twice. All four honour MRL at 768. The norms do not agree with each other at all:

| model | native | native norm | @768 norm | upstreams | repeats |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `google/gemini-embedding-2` | 3072 | 1.000000 | **1.000000** | 4 (all Google) | byte-identical |
| `google/gemini-embedding-001` | 3072 | 1.000000 | **0.597891** | 2 (Google, AI Studio) | byte-identical |
| `qwen/qwen3-embedding-8b` | 4096 | 1.000000 | 1.000000 | 3 (Nebius, DeepInfra, SiliconFlow/fp8) | cosine **0.999922** |
| `perplexity/pplx-embed-v1-4b` | **2560** | **15.900969** | 9.015750 | 1 (Perplexity/int8) | byte-identical |

Three findings. (1) **gemini-2 renormalises its own truncations and 001 does not** — 1.000000 against 0.597891 on the same request shape, which confirms Google's manual-normalisation instruction for 001 empirically *and* shows it does not generalise to the newer model. The reason `dimensions` stays unplumbed is therefore no longer "truncations are non-unit"; it is that **truncations are non-unit on half the catalogue and `storeEmbeddings` cannot tell which model it is holding**. (2) **pplx-embed is non-unit at its NATIVE width, by a factor of 16** — the card said unnormalised and understated it. Verified harmless: `similarityFromEmbeddings` (`shared/mmr.ts`) normalises on the way in before its dot-product loop, and `loadLibraryMatrix` L2-normalises every row at load, so the two JS paths that could have assumed unit length do not. (3) **Multi-upstream routing does NOT by itself mix spaces, and this is the finding that sharpens [F-038](#f-038).** qwen is the only model whose repeats are not byte-identical, and it is the one served by **three separate companies at differing quantization** (Nebius, DeepInfra, SiliconFlow at fp8) — yet the two repeats agree to cosine **0.999922**, and its truncations to 0.999929. That is float noise; it reorders nothing. So the qwen row in the evaluation archive **is** a fact about the model, and the reflex to suspect routing was wrong.

The correction it forces is worth more than the reassurance. F-038's mixture was **not** "different hosts compute different vectors" — measured here, they do not, even across three vendors and a quantization difference. It was one host **honouring a request field** and another **dropping it**, which is a far narrower failure with a far narrower remedy. `requiresProviderPin` firing only for a `parameter` mode is therefore correctly scoped, not under-scoped: with no field in dispute there is nothing for routing to disagree about. An earlier draft of this entry asserted the opposite and was wrong.

**Two lessons about the instrument, both of which cost a wrong conclusion first.** Upstream count is a **risk signal, not a verdict** — gemini-2 has four and is byte-identical, pplx has one. And byte-equality is the **wrong measurement to report**: the probe's first version printed only "REPEAT DISAGREED", and 0.9999 (noise) against 0.85 (two spaces) mean opposite things while looking identical in that output. It prints the cosine now, and the upstream list beside it.

## F-098

**A provider package's major version does not track the `ai` major, and the mismatch is a runtime throw on a path nothing tests.**

Found while auditing whether AI SDK v6/v7 was worth migrating to. `packages/core` declares `"@ai-sdk/openai-compatible": "^2.0.0"`, which pnpm resolved to **2.0.10** — a package that depends on `@ai-sdk/provider@3.0.3` and returns an embedding model declaring `specificationVersion = "v3"`. `ai@5.0.118`'s `resolveEmbeddingModel` (`ai/dist/index.js:555`) throws `UnsupportedModelVersionError` for anything that is not `"v2"`, and both `embed` and `embedMany` route through it. So selecting the OpenAI-Compatible provider for the Embeddings role threw on its first batch — a self-hosted operator's most likely choice, broken with no test covering it.

**The version map, which is the whole trap.** `@ai-sdk/openai-compatible` **1.0.0–1.0.53** is the line built on `@ai-sdk/provider@2`, i.e. the one `ai@5` accepts; **2.0.x** (from 2025-12-22, the day `ai@6.0.0` shipped) is already the v6 line. The package major is one behind the SDK major it targets, so a `^2.0.0` range on an `ai@5` app reads as conservative and is not. Corrected to `^1.0.53`, verified resolving to `@ai-sdk/provider@2.0.3` and `EmbeddingModelV2`. The same off-by-one holds across the family — `@ai-sdk/openai@2.x` is the v5 line, `4.x` is v7 — so **a range must be checked against the resolved `@ai-sdk/provider` major, never against the package's own version looking sensible.**

**Hugging Face was never wired at all, and the catalogue claimed otherwise.** `@ai-sdk/huggingface` types `textEmbeddingModel(modelId): never` — the method throws by construction — in `1.0.13` and still in `2.0.41`, where only the newer `embeddingModel()` works. Yet `huggingface.json` carried `"supportsEmbeddings": true`, so `getProvidersForFunction('embeddings')` offered it and any selection failed. Set to `false`: the honest statement is that this provider has no embeddings on the SDK line this app is on, which is the same discipline as [F-097](#f-097)'s "absent is a positive fact".

**Why neither surfaced.** Both are runtime failures on a provider nobody here uses, reached only by an admin changing the Embeddings role; `pnpm validate` cannot see them because every branch of `getEmbeddingInvocation`'s switch is cast `as any` — a deliberate escape from nine incompatible provider types that also erases the one signal that would have caught this. The generalisable rule is that **the `as any` in that switch buys provider-agnosticism at the cost of spec-version checking**, so a range bump there needs the resolved provider major read by hand.

## F-099

**A reasoning model bills its scratchpad from the answer's allowance, and until now the only lever was to buy more allowance.**

[F-030](#f-030) records the explanations path measuring 2,079 and 2,283 reasoning tokens against a 3,000 ceiling, answered by raising the large-context ceiling to **16,000**; [F-064](#f-064) records the identical failure in the title-analysis writer, answered by `analysisMaxOutputTokens` defaulting to **8,000**. Both treat the symptom. The cause is that a reasoning model deliberates before it writes, and both roles are batch work producing short structured prose from data already assembled for them — the shape of task that needs the least deliberation and pays for it per item, across a whole library.

**The lever already existed and nothing used it.** `@ai-sdk/google@2.0.52` (installed) takes `providerOptions.google.thinkingConfig.thinkingLevel`; `@openrouter/ai-sdk-provider@1.5.4` (installed) takes `providerOptions.openrouter.reasoning.effort`. Both on the versions already in the lockfile, on the two providers these roles actually run on. Before this change the repo passed `providerOptions` to exactly **one** call in the tree — the embedding `taskType` — and never to a text generation. This was briefly written up as a reason to migrate to AI SDK v7, whose provider-agnostic top-level `reasoning` option covers the same ground; checking the installed packages first showed v7 would have bought **portability, not capability**, on a feature reachable that afternoon.

**THE FIRST VERSION SHIPPED A FIXED VOCABULARY AND WAS WRONG THREE WAYS.** It defined `minimal | low | medium | high` as a union, offered it on an allowlist of two *providers*, and mapped it per provider. Measured against OpenRouter's live `/api/v1/models` (418 entries, 2026-09-01), an effort vocabulary is a property of the **model**:

| | count |
|---|---|
| models with no `reasoning` object at all | 124 |
| models that reason but expose **no effort parameter** (`deepseek-r1`, `gemini-2.5-flash`) | 140 |
| models publishing `reasoning.supported_efforts` | 147 |
| **distinct vocabularies among those 147** | **21** |
| distinct words across all of them | 7 |
| models offering all 7 | **0** |

Anthropic's models take `max` and reject `minimal`. OpenAI's take `xhigh` and `none`. Google's take `minimal` and neither of the others. `sakana/fugu-ultra` takes exactly `max, xhigh, high`. So a fixed union is wrong in three directions at once — it **offers words the model rejects** (`minimal` to Anthropic, a 400 on a batch job), **hides words it accepts** (`none` is the strongest form of the thing this feature exists to do, and was unreachable), and **cannot grow** when a vendor adds one. The provider allowlist was wrong the same way one level up: 264 of OpenRouter's 418 models take no effort, so the control rendered for models that have none.

**The native-Google half was wrong differently, and worse.** `thinkingLevel` reaches the wire as `thinking_level`, a **Gemini 3.x** field. Gemini 2.5 uses `thinkingBudget` — a token count, not a level — and Gemini 1.5 has no thinking at all. Of the seven models in `google.json`, four are 3.x and **three would have 400'd**. A provider-level guard cannot see that; only a per-model declaration can.

**Rebuilt so the vocabulary is data.** `openrouter-capabilities.ts` already fetched and cached `/api/v1/models` daily with a `CACHE_VERSION` designed to be bumped, so `supported_efforts` rides in beside pricing and context length (version 2 → 3). `getModelsForFunctionWithCustom` stamps it onto each model's metadata — the same enrichment path that already fixed pricing, and the only one that works, since OpenRouter's chat models are all user-entered and no static file could know what an operator typed. Native Google declares `reasoningMechanism: "thinkingLevel"` per model in the catalogue JSON, where the SDK's own `z.enum` fixes the four words. The dropdown renders the selected model's list; the settings route validates against **the same call**; the request builder re-checks per call.

**Eight rules, each the safe side of an asymmetry.**

1. **Absent means send nothing.** No default effort, so every role stored before this field existed builds a byte-identical request. Which effort suits a given model is unmeasured here, and this repo does not threshold blind.
2. **Absent also means the model takes none.** `reasoningMechanism` undefined is a positive fact, never a default to fall through — the exact discipline [F-097](#f-097) had to retrofit onto `inputTypeMechanism` after its `'parameter'` default sent a mode to models that read none.
3. **Nothing is rounded.** An effort the model does not list is refused and nothing is sent, leaving the provider default. Mapping `minimal` onto `low` would leave the settings page and the wire disagreeing about what was asked for — the class of fault [F-093](#f-093) records — and the silent direction of that error is *cheaper thinking than asked for*, which surfaces as quality nobody can trace. Falling back errs toward **more** thinking: a cost an operator can see.
4. **`KNOWN_REASONING_EFFORTS` is a display order and a label-key set, never a filter.** OpenRouter's field is an open string; a word added tomorrow is offered as-is, sorted last, labelled with the vendor's own English name. Filtering would hide a capability the model genuinely has, which is the bug being fixed.
5. **The roles stay an allowlist** (`textGeneration`, `titleAnalysis`). A role storing an effort nothing reads is a control that appears to work while no request changes, and the only trace is a bill that did not move. `chat` is excluded on purpose — it is interactive, a reader is watching, and thinking is often the point.
6. **The namespace key must be the provider id.** The SDK hands `providerOptions.<id>` to that provider and silently ignores every other key, so a mechanism/provider mismatch is not an error — it is a setting that saves, displays and does nothing. `MECHANISM_PROVIDER` is a table, and a test asserts every delivered payload's sole key is the provider it was resolved for.
7. **The title-analysis writer resolves per ATTEMPT, against provider *and* model.** A fallback may live on a different provider, and two models on one provider need not share a vocabulary. Getting either wrong leaves the scratchpad quietly uncapped on exactly the fallback path that already means something has gone wrong.
8. **Validation happens at save AND at send.** OpenRouter's catalogue moves under stored settings, and switching models is the ordinary way to end up off-list. The route refuses an unlistable word; the request builder re-checks and warns rather than sending it; and the dropdown re-adds an off-list stored value so a MUI Select cannot render blank and have the next save write that blank over a real setting.

**`@openrouter/ai-sdk-provider`'s `effort` type is a stale narrow annotation, not an enforcement.** It is declared `'high' | 'medium' | 'low'` in `index.d.ts`, but `doGenerate` spreads `providerOptions.openrouter` **verbatim** into the request body with no schema in the way — the same mechanism [F-038](#f-038) records for `extraBody` on the embeddings side — so `xhigh`, `max`, `minimal` and `none` all reach the wire. `@ai-sdk/google` is the opposite and must be read that way: `thinkingLevel` is validated against a real `z.enum` before the request is built, so an off-list value **throws locally** rather than reaching Google. One provider's type is advisory and the other's is load-bearing; the code treats them differently because they are different.

**The three router pseudo-models are the interesting negative.** `openrouter/auto`, `/free` and `/auto-beta` declare `reasoning_effort` in `supported_parameters` and carry **no** `reasoning` object. That is correct of them — they pick a real model per request, so no vocabulary is knowable in advance — and it is why `supported_parameters` is not the field to read. They land on "no efforts" and get no control, which is the right answer rather than a gap.

**MEASURED, and the feature does what it claims.** `scripts/probe-reasoning-effort.mjs` against `google/gemini-3.5-flash`, two runs per level:

| effort | reasoning tokens |
|---|---|
| `minimal` | **0 / 0** |
| `low` | 765 / 768 |
| `medium` | 1033 / 918 |
| `high` | 1476 / 1529 |
| *(no effort sent)* | 1084 / 897 |
| `aperture_not_a_level` (control) | **rejected, HTTP 400** |

Four things settle at once. (1) Every catalogued word is accepted and the invented one is **refused**, so `supported_efforts` is a real capability read and not decoration — without that control the other three rows prove nothing. (2) The level is **honoured**, not merely accepted: 0 to ~1,500 tokens is a 1,500-token swing in the same allowance the prose is billed from, which is precisely [F-030](#f-030)'s and [F-064](#f-064)'s failure being paid for at every level except the lowest. (3) `minimal` reasons **not at all** — for a batch role writing short structured prose from data already assembled, that is the setting the feature exists to reach, and it was unreachable while the ceiling was the only lever. (4) The no-effort baseline (~990) lands on `medium`, matching the catalogue's own `default_effort: "medium"` — so the catalogue is accurate about the default as well as the vocabulary, and every role shipped before this was silently buying the middle rung.

The repeats are what make the middle rungs readable: `high` varied by 53 tokens between its own two runs, so a single sample could not have separated `medium` from `high`, though it would have been enough for `minimal` against anything. This is the instrument lesson [F-097](#f-097) paid for, applied at a cost of one extra call per level.

**The probe's own first version was the same mistake in miniature.** It defaulted to three models from three vendors — chosen to watch their vocabularies disagree — which is ~35 paid generations to re-establish a fact that reading `/api/v1/models` already settles for free. The operator paid for two of them before stopping it. The default is now the model a reasoning-reading role is **actually configured with**, its weakest and strongest levels only, plus the control: **six calls**, and it refuses to run at all when no role is on OpenRouter. `--all-levels` buys the full ladder above. A verification script bills the person running it, so its default scope is a design decision with a price, not a thoroughness setting.

**What remains per-model.** That gemini honours the field is not a promise that every model does; a model may accept a level and ignore it, which makes the setting cosmetic *for that model* — nothing breaks and no answer room is bought. Six calls answers it for whatever a role is pointed at, which is the only model whose answer matters.

**Deliberately not built:** `thinkingBudget` for Gemini 2.5. Turning an effort word into a token count is a number nobody has measured, and this repo does not threshold blind. Those models declare no mechanism and get no control — honest, and visibly so.

### F-100

**A tool result's `reason` was the model's scratchpad, and a chat answer was rendered in an order nobody produced.** One turn, two faults, both visible on the same page.

**The scratchpad.** `enrichCardReasons` set `maxOutputTokens = chunk.length * 120 + 200`. The Text Generation role was `nvidia/nemotron-3-ultra-550b-a55b:free`, whose OpenRouter entry reads `supported_efforts: ["high","medium"], default_effort: "high"` — an "open frontier-reasoning" model that **always** reasons and cannot be asked to reason less than medium. A reasoning model bills its scratchpad from the same output allowance as its prose (the same mechanism as F-030's explanation ceiling and F-064's analysis default), so at 680 tokens for four titles it spent the budget planning and the plan became the answer, cut off mid-word. Printed on a card, under a lightbulb icon: *"No research note provided, only synopsis. This seems like a British fantasy/horror film. I need to check if it's actually inspired by Nosferatu. The synopsis doesn't suggest it. But the user included it in the list, so I must write something. However, the rule says "Never invent facts. If y"*.

Three things worth keeping. (1) **The deliberation was CORRECT** — it caught a candidate that does not belong and identified the tension between two instructions. The failure was budget, not judgment, so "use a non-reasoning model" is the wrong lesson; **a 4000-token floor** fixed it outright (measured next run: `items 16, rewritten 16, missing 0`, and the notes read as notes). A cap is not a reservation, so the headroom is free on a model that answers straight away. (2) **`isPresentableReason` is the guard**, pure and pinned against the exact strings that shipped: a line narrating the model's own task, or ending in the truncation ellipsis, is dropped and the card keeps its original web note. It fails toward the shorter honest sentence because the directions are not symmetric — a rejection costs an adjective, a false accept prints an inner monologue. Second person is deliberately **not** a marker; "you'll recognise the silhouette" is the reader being addressed. (3) **`BATCH_SIZE` 8 → 4 was a regression and is reverted**: measured 115s → **135s**. Latency here is per CALL, not per token, because the fixed cost of thinking is paid once per request — halving the chunk doubles the number of requests paying it.

**The order.** See the F-023 entry: `parts JSONB` (`0158`) records the sequence an answer was produced in, and the hoist is gone.

**Two instrument gaps found on the way.** `resolveCandidates` logged **nothing**, so a turn that shipped 4 cards out of 11 web picks was indistinguishable in the log from one that found 4 — the only way to tell was counting posters in a screenshot. It now records `candidates/matched/notInLibrary/droppedAsWatched`, `matched` being the number the model itself reads when deciding whether to supplement. And its Tier 3 year guard was `if (cand.year && r.year && …)`, so **a candidate with no year matched anything sharing the title**: "Beauty and the Beast", researched as Cocteau's 1946 film, claimed Disney's 1991 one — exact-title rank beats `La Belle et la Bête` — and carried the Cocteau note onto its card. A yearless candidate against more than one free row is now ambiguous and claims nothing.

## F-101

**A global security header switched off a whole feature, and every visible clue pointed away from it.** Trailers rendered as YouTube's own dark panel: *"Video player configuration error / Error 153"*, on every title, in every one of the four places `TrailerModal` is mounted.

**Everything that normally explains a dead embed was already correct.** The frame was not blocked — the error page is served *by YouTube, inside the frame*, so `frameSrc 'self' https://www.youtube.com https://www.youtube-nocookie.com` was doing its job and the console showed no CSP violation. The embed URL was well-formed (`youtubeWatchUrlToEmbedUrl` validates the eleven-character id and is pinned by its own regex). The video was public and embeddable. `allow` carried `autoplay`. COEP is off; CORP governs responses this server sends, not a frame's own navigation. Every artefact a reader would reach for first was innocent, which is why the cause is worth writing down: it is in `apps/api/src/config/security.ts`, a file with nothing to do with trailers.

**The cause is `referrerPolicy: { policy: 'no-referrer' }` in `helmetOptions()`.** YouTube's embedded player uses the `Referer` header to identify the embedding site, and around September 2025 it began refusing to build a player configuration without one — Error 153 is that refusal. The symptom is not specific to this app; it was reported across WordPress, Discourse, ServiceNow and Electron shells over the same period, and the common factor in every case is a context that sends no referrer: a `no-referrer` response header, a `<meta name="referrer">`, a privacy extension stripping it, or a sandboxed webview. Here it is the response header, which is a deliberate and correct default for the rest of the app.

**MEASURED, both directions, under the app's real headers.** `scripts/probe-yt-referrer.mjs` serves one page with Aperture's exact `Referrer-Policy` and CSP and embeds `https://www.youtube.com/embed/<id>?autoplay=1&mute=1&rel=0` once, with the iframe attribute switched by a query parameter:

| iframe | result |
|---|---|
| no `referrerpolicy` (inherits the document's `no-referrer`) | **"Video player configuration error / Error 153"** — pixel-identical to the report |
| `referrerpolicy="strict-origin-when-cross-origin"` | **plays** — player chrome, title bar, video frames |

Same document, same header, same CSP, same video id, same session. The attribute is the only variable, so this is a fix rather than a guess — which matters here because the deployment loop is push → image build → operator pull, and a wrong guess costs a full round trip to discover.

**The fix goes on the element, not on the policy.** A request's referrer policy is taken from the element's `referrerpolicy` attribute when it has one and from the document's only otherwise, so a single frame can opt out without the site doing so. Loosening `helmetOptions()` to `strict-origin-when-cross-origin` would also have worked and is the wrong trade: it would send this instance's origin on *every* outbound request — TMDB posters loaded direct from `image.tmdb.org`, the Google Fonts stylesheet, every link a user opens — to fix the one cross-origin frame in the app. `security.test.ts`'s assertion that the header is `no-referrer` therefore stays green, which is the tell that the change was correctly scoped.

**`strict-origin-when-cross-origin` rather than `origin`** because it withholds the referrer on an HTTPS → HTTP downgrade while still sending it on HTTP → HTTPS, so a LAN install served over plain HTTP still reaches YouTube. It is also the browser default, so the frame ends up behaving exactly as it would on a site with no policy at all.
