**Verified through MUI, not only in a bare iframe.** The element is `Box component="iframe"`, and emotion filters props for string tags, so "the attribute works" and "the attribute is emitted" are two claims. `renderToStaticMarkup` on that Box emits `<iframe … referrerPolicy="strict-origin-when-cross-origin" allowfullscreen="">`, so it survives the wrapper. Worth checking rather than assuming: a filtered prop would have produced the identical Error 153 with a correct-looking source file.

**Two things deliberately not done.** Switching the embed host to `youtube-nocookie.com` is reported as a fix elsewhere and is already permitted by `frameSrc`, but it is not what changed and was not measured here; shipping it alongside would have made the working configuration ambiguous. And no test pins the attribute — `packages/ui` has no test runner, and adding one plus a DOM for a single assertion is a larger change than the fix. The comment at the attribute carries the reason instead, written to be read by whoever next tidies "redundant" iframe props.

## F-102

**The stats page could count a population but never show it, and its one existing link answered a different question than the badge beside it.** Every chart on Watch Stats is a bucket over watch history — a genre, a decade, a rating band, an actor, a month, an hour of the week — and each rendered its size and stopped there. The four ranked lists were the near miss: a row did navigate, but to `/person/:name` or `/studio/:name`, which lists **everything in the library** that person or studio touched. Sitting one line under a chip reading *"7 films"*, that page shows dozens, and nothing on it marks which seven the chip meant.

**The count and the list must be the same SQL text, not the same intent.** `GET /api/users/:id/watch-stats/breakdown` resolves a bucket to a predicate over the same population the summary counted, and `watchStatsFilters.ts` holds the two predicates both routes read (`WATCHED_SQL`, `LIBRARY_ENABLED_SQL`). A restated predicate would drift silently and in the least forgivable way there is: a "7 films" badge that opens five is a bug report, not a rounding difference. Same reasoning as `needsEnrichmentSql` (F-014) and `watchedExclusion.ts` (F-033) — one home, both callers.

**Two summary cards deliberately relax it.** Favorites is counted with no watched predicate at all (a bookmark is a favorite whether or not it was ever played) and rewatched keys on `play_count > 1` rather than on having been played, so `skipWatched` exists on the resolver and those two dimensions set it. Without it, the tile opens a shorter list than the number it was clicked from — the exact failure the shared predicate was written to prevent, reintroduced one card at a time.

**An unparseable value is a 400, never an empty list.** A dimension the route does not know, or a decade label it cannot read, would otherwise render as *"Nothing to show here"* — which reads as a fact about the viewer's history rather than as a fault, and is therefore the one wrong answer that never gets reported.

**Labels are for the reader; keys are for the filter.** The timeline's x-axis is `to_char(month, 'Mon YYYY')` and the heatmap's tooltip is a localized weekday, so neither can be sent back as a filter without the server parsing a month name in fifteen languages. The timeline query now also emits `to_char(month, 'YYYY-MM')` as `monthKey`, and the heatmap sends its raw `(dow, hour)`. The label still travels, but only as the dialog's heading — so the heading is the same words the reader clicked rather than a second description of it.

**The heatmap and busiest-day counts are measured without the library filter, so their drill-ins can come back one or two short.** Both aggregate every `watch_history` row with a `last_played_at`, while the drill-in applies `LIBRARY_ENABLED_SQL`. Left as-is: the drill-in is the more correct of the two, and a title in a disabled library appearing in a list the viewer cannot open would be worse than a count that is high by one.

**`seriesGenreDistribution` was queried on every load and rendered nowhere.** It was in the response, in the TypeScript interface, and in no JSX — so the page ran a `jsonb`-free but still real aggregate over every watched series and threw it away, and stayed silent about half of what gets watched. It now renders as a ranked bar list next to the movie genre donut, which is also the only place the two are comparable.

**Four copies of one list is how two of them ended up wired differently.** Actors, directors, studios and networks were four ~100-line blocks differing in colour, avatar shape and count label; `StatRankList` is the one copy. The same shape produced the duplicate this collapsed: average community rating rendered *twice*, once as a highlight tile and once as the hero number of "Your Taste vs. the Crowd" five sections below it — the detail page's "state each fact once" rule, on a page nobody had applied it to.

**Where a card goes is a claim about what it is.** "Your Taste vs. the Crowd" is a metric, so it sits in the metric band rather than under two rows of charts; the busiest day and the rewatch count are metrics too, so they join the tile strip instead of being a second, differently-shaped highlights row. The gradient tiles are gone — six full-bleed gradients gave six numbers of unequal importance the same shout, and left nothing on the page able to be emphasised.

**The identity has two hosts now, so it has one implementation.** `WatcherIdentityCard` (synopsis, streaming generate, four figures) was the Watcher Identity settings tab's Section 4; it is now a component both that tab and the stats page mount. The settings tab keeps the knobs that shape it — refresh interval, franchise thresholds, custom interests, library exclusions — because those are settings and the account of your taste is not. Copying the SSE reader into a second page would have drifted the first time the stream format moved.

**The drill-in opens a title in place.** The page mounts `MediaDetailModalProvider`, so clicking a poster inside the breakdown dialog stacks the detail dialog above it rather than routing away and losing the chart the reader came from. A person's full filmography is still one click further out, as an explicit link in the dialog header — the dialog answers *"which of these did I watch"*, and that link answers *"what else is there"*.

## F-103

**Account assumption is a second cookie, never a swapped session — and it is read-only because a method allowlist is the only promise that survives the next handler someone adds.**

**The shape.** An admin viewing the app as another user gets an `aperture_impersonation` cookie *beside* their own `aperture_session`, backed by a row in `impersonation_sessions` (`0159`) holding `admin_user_id`, `admin_session_id`, `target_user_id` and a one-hour `expires_at`. The auth plugin resolves the session first, then layers the assumption on top: `request.user` becomes the target — so every one of the app's several hundred handlers sees the assumed account and needs no knowledge of the feature — and `request.impersonation` holds the admin who is really there.

**The alternative was to mint a session for the target and replace the cookie, and it cannot be undone.** `sessions.token_hash` stores only a digest, exactly so a stolen database cannot be replayed; that is also why the admin's original token is unrecoverable once the cookie is overwritten. The exit would then have to *re-mint* a session — a fresh login the admin never performed, with a new `last_login_at`, and a failure mode where a database error mid-exit leaves them holding a target session and no way home. Layering instead means the admin's own credential is untouched in the browser the whole time: stopping is a delete of one row and one cookie, and even a *failed* stop leaves them exactly where they were rather than half-exited. The client leans on that — `stopImpersonation` reloads in a `finally`, because the worst case of a failed call is that the banner is still there.

**Nothing writes to the target, and that is a promise with a shape.** No session row is created for them, so `sessions.last_seen_at` never moves; no login happens, so `users.last_login_at` and `users.updated_at` stay as the target left them. But the interesting half is everything else, because ordinary browsing writes constantly: `ViewModeProvider` PATCHes a view mode the moment a grid is toggled, the assistant persists a conversation per turn, a heart writes `user_ratings`, and the user-actions menu can start jobs on their behalf. Auditing each of those is a snapshot, not a rule — the next handler added is unaudited by construction. So an assumed session is refused every unsafe HTTP method (`impersonationBlocksRequest`, `lib/impersonation.ts`, pure and pinned), which is a property of the *transport* and therefore holds for handlers nobody has written yet. It is also the honest description of what "see what they see" means.

**The allowlist has two entries and both END the assumption**: `POST /api/auth/impersonate/stop` and `POST /api/auth/logout`. An admin who cannot POST anything cannot get back out, so the exemption is not a convenience. Note what is *not* exempt: `POST /api/auth/impersonate` sits one path segment above the stop route, so the exact-match set refuses it and assumptions cannot be chained — there is always exactly one account to return to, never a stack.

**Two POSTs had to be let through, and finding them was the only part method-shape got wrong.** `/api/favorites/status/bulk` and `/api/seerr/status/batch` are POSTs solely because a list of ids does not fit in a query string; both are pure reads. Blocking them left the assumed session rendering a library with no favourite hearts and no request badges — the app displaying *wrongly*, in a mode whose entire purpose is seeing it display as that user sees it. They are a separate set from the exit routes so the two reasons stay distinguishable, and the bar for joining them is that the handler writes nothing for anyone. Everything else surveyed came out clean on method alone: `getUserUiPreferences` is a pure read and only `updateUserUiPreferences` (PATCH) inserts the row, `getUserTasteProfile` — which rebuilds and stores — is reached only from `POST /api/settings/taste-profile/rebuild`, and every write in `routes/watching` is a POST or DELETE.

**Four checks re-run on every request rather than once at the start**, because a grant outlives the state that justified it: the admin must still be an admin (a demotion ends their assumptions immediately), the grant must still be presented by the session that created it (signing out and back in orphans it), it must not have lapsed, and the target must still be enabled — the same rule sessions already apply, so a disabled account is not browsable by anyone, through any door. A grant is also `ON DELETE CASCADE` from `sessions(id)`, so `deleteAllUserSessions` (which `PUT /api/users/:id` calls when disabling an account) takes it with the admin's session, and `cleanup-auth-state` sweeps lapsed rows. That sweep deletes on the exact deadline, unlike the session sweep's deliberate one-day grace margin: the auth plugin already refuses a lapsed grant before the job ever sees it, so there is no live state for a boundary disagreement to cost.

**A database hiccup must not end an assumption, and must not extend one.** The two failure paths are deliberately asymmetric. If the *session* lookup throws, `request.sessionError` is set and the impersonation cookie is left alone — a transient error is not evidence that the assumption is over. If the *grant* lookup throws, the admin stays themselves rather than falling back to the target, because being wrongly yourself is recoverable and being wrongly someone else is not.

**The exit is in the app bar, which is the only place "on every page" is structural.** `ImpersonationBanner` renders above the `<Toolbar>` inside `Layout`'s fixed `AppBar` — every authenticated route in `App.tsx` goes through that one shell, so no page can forget to include the way out. It is above the toolbar rather than inside it because the toolbar's contents are about the *page* and this is about *who you currently are*. Three further exits exist behind it, in descending order of how much has to be working: signing out (allowlisted, and it clears both cookies), the one-hour lease, and the fact that the admin's own session cookie is still in the browser. The banner shows the remaining minutes for that reason — knowing there is an automatic end is the difference between a mode and a trap.

**The banner's height is fixed at 44px because four layout calculations subtract it.** `Layout` publishes `--aperture-chrome-top` (64, or 108 while assuming) on the shell element, and `<main>`'s `mt`/`minHeight`, `pages/assistant`'s `calc(100vh - … - 32/48px)`, `pages/explore`'s full-height box and `AdminShell`'s sticky nav rail all read it with a `64px` fallback. A variable rather than a prop because those sit inside the outlet and would each need their own copy of the arithmetic — four places for it to go stale. A banner that sized itself to its content would make every one of them a guess.

**Crossing the boundary is a full page load, in both directions.** The identity is baked into a provider tree and three localStorage caches (`aperture_watching_cache`, `aperture-view-modes`, `aperture-poster-prefs`), none of which is namespaced by user id — invisible while a browser only ever holds one identity, and immediately wrong here, where the admin's cached "Shows You Watch" list would render as the target's until a background fetch corrected it. Swapping identity in place means finding every provider that reads once on mount and inventing a reset for it, and being wrong about one of them shows the admin their own data while claiming to be someone else. A reload is the one move that cannot leave a survivor. The three keys moved into `lib/clientCaches.ts` rather than being re-listed: a provider module exporting a plain constant beside its component trips `react-refresh/only-export-components`, and lint runs at `--max-warnings 0`.

**Impersonating another admin is allowed.** It grants nothing the caller did not already have — both are admins, and the session is read-only regardless — so refusing it would be a rule with no property behind it. What it does mean is that `/admin/*` stays reachable in that one case, which is why the nav rail's viewport arithmetic reads the chrome variable too.

### F-103 addendum — what the security audit changed

**The method rule was right and incomplete: two GETs write, and reading handlers would not have found them.** A transitive sweep of the call graph (route handler → core function → SQL) turned up exactly two paths where a GET reaches a write to user-scoped state, and both had been read past.

**`GET /api/trakt/auth-url` + `GET /api/trakt/callback` are one cross-account write split over two GETs.** `auth-url` mints a one-time state token into an in-memory map, bound to `request.user.id` — the *target* during an assumption. `callback` is unauthenticated by design (it is an OAuth redirect) and trusts that state, calling `storeUserTraktTokens(stateData.userId, …)`. So an admin viewing the app as someone else could complete a Trakt handshake and attach **their own** Trakt account to the **target's** Aperture account, using nothing but GETs — and the next `syncAllTraktRatings` would push that user's ratings to the admin's Trakt profile. The write outlives the assumption entirely; ending the session does not unlink it. `BLOCKED_GETS` names both. The callback is blocked even when its state may name the admin rather than the target (a flow started before the assumption): failing that way costs one retry after exiting, and failing the other way is not recoverable.

**`getUserSettings` was a getter that wrote, and it was on the hottest read path in the app.** It ran `INSERT INTO user_settings (user_id) … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` whenever the row was absent, and it is reached from `GET /api/auth/me/preferences` — every page load — plus `resolveEffectiveUiLanguage`, `resolveEffectiveAiLanguage` and `getEffectiveAiExplanationSetting`. So the first page of an assumed session materialised a row for a target who had never had one, stamped `created_at`/`updated_at` with a time that user did nothing at. Harmless in behaviour (an absent row and a defaults row mean the same thing to every reader) and a straight violation of the promise. It is a pure read now: the function already carried a "this shouldn't happen, return defaults" branch, which is simply the correct answer, and both writers that need the row (`updateUserSettings`, `updateUserUiPreferences`) do their own `INSERT … ON CONFLICT` first. **The general rule this leaves: a getter that writes defeats a method-based guard silently, and the only way to find one is to trace the call graph rather than to read the handlers.**

**Two smaller hardenings, neither exploitable, both removing a primitive rather than a bug.** A non-admin presenting a grant token used to `DELETE` the row — a cross-session write handed to the wrong party, and inconsistent with the session-mismatch branch immediately below it, which deliberately does not delete. Clearing that browser's own cookie is the entire remedy, since every request re-checks `isAdmin` and the row is inert. And the stale-cookie clear is now scoped to `/api/` requests, so a `Set-Cookie` is not stapled to every static asset response — those are the ones an intermediary might cache, and the SPA cannot render without calling the API, so the clear still always lands.

**The framework contract is pinned, because its failure is invisible.** Everything rests on an async `onRequest` hook that replies aborting the lifecycle. If a Fastify upgrade ever relaxed that, the guard would become advisory: the response would still be a 403 while the handler had already written the row. `impersonation.test.ts` boots a real Fastify instance and asserts the handler does not run.

**What the audit cleared.** Registration order is correct — `authPlugin` is registered before `routes` and before `@fastify/static`, so the hook is on every route including the SPA fallback (a route registered *before* the hook would not have it, which is the ordering bug this class invites). CSRF is covered twice over: `SameSite=Lax` on both cookies, and a CORS origin callback that only reflects `APP_BASE_URL` with `credentials: true`. The path matcher fails closed on every mangling tried — encoded separators, double slashes, matrix parameters, case changes — because the exempt sets are exact matches and anything unrecognised is refused; the trailing-slash tolerance is free, since Fastify's default `ignoreTrailingSlash: false` 404s those anyway. No method-override plugin is installed. The grant is not a standalone bearer token: it is useless without the admin session cookie it is bound to, so stealing it alone buys nothing. And no raw SQL write to a user-scoped table sits under a GET declaration anywhere in `apps/api/src/routes`.

**Residual risks, accepted and stated.** An assumed session can read the target's private assistant conversations, which no admin route exposes — that is an expansion of what an admin can see, inherent to the feature rather than a defect in it. The audit trail is `request.log.info` to stdout only, so it lives as long as the container's logs; a durable table would be the improvement if account assumption ever needs to answer to anyone. Instance-level caches (TMDB posters, JustWatch charts) are still warmed by GETs during an assumption — deliberate, since they are not the target's state. And the impersonation cookie carries no `__Host-` prefix, matching the session cookie beside it; both would be shadowable from a hostile sibling subdomain, which is a property of the existing session design rather than of this feature.

### Addendum: what the deployed page showed

Four things only visible against a real instance, at the width it is actually read at.

**MUI's `lg` was never reached, so half the responsive work was dead.** The deployed pane measured **1040 CSS px** — above `md` (900), below `lg` (1200). The tile strip was keyed `xs=6 sm=4 lg=3`, so it rendered three-up and left a two-tile orphan row, and the two identity cards keyed `lg=6` stacked full-width instead of pairing. Neither is a bug a build or a test can see. This is the same family as the existing "breakpoints lie inside the main pane" rule but a simpler instance of it: **`lg` is a big claim on a sidebar-and-dock layout, and `md` is where the wide arrangement has to start.** Both grids now key on `md`, and eight tiles divide evenly at four-up.

**The heatmap was 24 fixed 16px cells in a full-width card**, so it drew ~510px of instrument inside ~900px of card and read as if it were hiding in the corner. Cells now take their width from the column (`gridTemplateColumns: 'auto repeat(24, minmax(0, 1fr))'` with `aspectRatio: 1`), which is what lets it sit in half a row beside the activity chart and still be larger than it was at full width. **The hour axis has to be part of the same grid** — as a separate flex row it re-derives the column positions and drifts out of alignment with the cells above it.

**A card that stretches to its neighbour's height inherits its neighbour's mistakes.** "Where Your Hours Go" appeared to be rendering nothing: its donut was pushed below the fold because the card was height-matched to "Your Taste vs. the Crowd", which had grown tall for an unrelated reason — its left column had no `maxWidth`, so the caption *"Avg community rating of what you watch"* set the column to ~260px of a ~470px card and forced six guilty-pleasure chips into six rows. The fix is a fixed-width number column and a shorter label, not anything to do with the donut. Worth remembering when a chart looks broken in a `height: 100%` row: **check what it is being stretched by before checking the chart.**

**Both watcher identities ended mid-sentence** — *"You're drawn"*, *"gritty enough to feel"*. `tasteSynopsis.ts` and `tasteSeriesSynopsis.ts` both ask for a three-section profile and both set `maxOutputTokens: 400`. That is the fourth appearance of this mechanism ([F-030](#f-030) explanations, [F-064](#f-064) analysis, [F-100](#f-100) card reasons): a reasoning model bills its scratchpad from the same output allowance as its prose, so a budget sized for the prose alone buys a truncated answer. Raised to **4000** in both, mirrored per the parallel-implementations invariant. A cap is not a reservation, so this costs nothing on a model that answers directly.

**And the answer to "how far back does this go".** Every figure is all-time except the timeline, which is a rolling twelve months in the SQL itself. But the deeper limit is the table: `watch_history` is `UNIQUE (user_id, movie_id)` with `play_count` and `last_played_at` — **one row per title, not one row per play**. So the heatmap, the busiest day and the timeline are distributions of *each title's most recent play*, not of viewing events: a film watched five times contributes one point, at the date of the fifth. A title rewatched last week has therefore *moved out of* the month it was first watched in. Nothing on the page said any of this, so an empty early decade was ambiguous between "never watched" and "before we were looking". The response now carries `historySpan` (`MIN`/`MAX` of `last_played_at`) and the page opens with what it covers. Making the heatmap a true event distribution is not a display change — it needs a per-play table the media server may not even be able to backfill.

## F-104

**The Discovery pipeline's taste half, its ratings, and its pool.** Audit of `packages/core/src/discover/`, `apps/api/src/routes/discovery/`, and the `generate-discovery-suggestions` job, 2026-09-03.

### The similarity term is unreachable (FIXED — see the addendum at the end of this entry)

`scoreCandidates` builds `embeddingMap` by joining `movies`/`series` on `tmdb_id` against the active embedding table. `filterCandidates` has already removed every candidate whose `tmdb_id` appears in those tables. The join's match set is therefore a strict subset of the exclusion set — a row matching the join has `tmdb_id` exactly equal to the candidate's, which parses to an excluded id — so `embeddingMap` is empty on every run and `similarityScore` is the constant `0.5`.

This holds at all three call sites: both pipeline variants filtered before scoring, and `POST /:mediaType/expand` calls `filterCandidates` then `scoreCandidates`. `scorer.test.ts` passes because all seven original cases call `maxTasteSimilarity` directly with hand-built vectors; nothing tested that the pipeline supplies one.

Measured consequence — configured share of the blend (weights over their total of 1.1) against share of ranking *variance*, since a constant cannot order anything:

| Term | Configured | Actual |
|---|---:|---:|
| Taste similarity | 45.5% | 0% |
| Popularity | 27.3% | 50.0% |
| Recency | 18.2% | 33.3% |
| Source quality | 9.1% | 16.7% |

Also makes the `minSimilarity` UI filter inert: every stored row holds 0.5, so any threshold at or below it passes everything and anything above returns nothing.

**Not repaired**, because there is no cheap repair: candidates are by definition outside the library, so no stored vector exists for them. The options are materially different products — embed each candidate's TMDb text on the fly with the active model and pay per candidate; restrict scoring to candidates that have a vector from some other path; or delete the term and stop labelling a popularity ranking as a taste match.

### Popularity mixes three scales (FIXED — see the addendum at the end of this entry)

`calculatePopularityScore` min-max normalises `candidate.popularity` across the merged pool, but that field holds TMDb's unbounded popularity metric for the three TMDb sources, a Trakt watcher count (~10–500) for `trakt_trending`, and a hardcoded `0` for `trakt_popular` and `trakt_recommendations`. So the two sources the code labels most personalized score zero on the term that now carries half the ranking.

Worked with a neutral preference adjustment (all four affinities 0.5 make `netPull` zero, so `applyPreferenceAdjustment` returns the base score unchanged): a recent Trakt recommendation reaches **0.500**; a recent TMDb Discover title at the top of the popularity range reaches **0.727**. No personalized pick can outrank a merely popular one.

### Ratings: fetched and discarded, then overwritten (fixed)

Two independent mechanisms produced "some cards show a rating, others don't".

1. **A rank cutoff.** The badge renders on `voteAverage != null && voteAverage > 0` (`MediaPosterCard.tsx`). Trakt candidates are built with a hardcoded `voteAverage: 0`. `enrichBasicData` — which runs over every global candidate — calls `getMovieDetails`/`getTVDetails`, whose responses **do** carry `vote_average` and `vote_count`, but its return object carried only poster, backdrop, language and overview. Only `enrichFullData` copied the vote fields, and it runs on the top `maxEnrichedCandidates` (150) alone. So a Trakt-sourced title showed a rating iff it ranked top-150 for that user. Confirmable from the UI without the database: the detail popper refetches live TMDb and merges (`voteAverage: tmdb.voteAverage ?? base.voteAverage`), so the rating appears there.

   Fixed by carrying the two fields through `enrichBasicData` — no extra request, the call was already being made. The selection predicate also gained `|| !c.voteCount`: without it a pool row already clobbered to 0 comes back **with** a poster and language, so the first two clauses would never re-select it and existing bad rows could not heal.

2. **A clobbering write.** The pool upsert used `vote_average = COALESCE($n, vote_average)`. `COALESCE` guards NULL only, and the Trakt path supplies a literal `0`, so `COALESCE(0, 7.8)` wrote `0` over a good rating whenever a title was seen by Trakt but had dropped out of TMDb Discover's window that run. Same for `vote_count` and `popularity` — and popularity feeds the ranking. The `genres` assignment in the same statement **was** already guarded (`CASE WHEN $n::jsonb != '[]'::jsonb`); the three numeric columns were not. Fixed with `NULLIF(…, 0)` on both the insert values and the conflict clause, so 0 stores as NULL ("unknown") and a later sighting fills it in.

### Everything else repaired in the same pass

- **Cancellation.** Nothing in `discover/` polled `isJobCancelled`, on the longest-running job in the system by external call count. Now polled between media types, between users, and between enrichment batches (the loop where the minutes actually go). A cancelled run still stores what it already paid for; the executor skips `completeJob`, since `cancelJob` has already written the `job_runs` row.
- **Unbounded pool.** `clearOldPoolEntries` existed, was exported, and was called by nothing; `getPoolCandidates` read the whole table with no limit and merged it into every user's run. Now pruned in the job's phase 1 and read with `maxPoolCandidates` (3000) / `poolMaxAgeDays` (30), both on `DiscoveryConfig`. The prune's `INTERVAL '1 day' * $2` also gained an explicit `::int` — without it Postgres can refuse to infer the parameter's type.
- **Two pipelines.** `generateDiscoveryForUser` and `generateDiscoveryForUserWithPool` were ~200 lines each differing only in their first two steps; the scheduled job used one and the user-facing refresh used the other, which called the `@deprecated` `fetchAllCandidates` and re-fetched every global source for one user. Collapsed to one implementation that always reads the pool, with an empty-pool seed so a fresh instance still works. `fetchAllCandidates` deleted.
- **Non-atomic store.** `storeDiscoveryCandidates` committed a DELETE and then issued up to 1,000 single-row INSERTs, each with a catch that logged and continued — so a crash or an HTTP timeout on the refresh path left a half-populated page presented as complete. Now one transaction, chunked at 200 rows (31 columns × 1000 = 31,000 bind parameters is under Postgres's 65,535 ceiling but needlessly close to it).
- **Pool enrichment cache.** The columns, `is_enriched`, and a single-row updater all existed and nothing called them, so every user re-fetched details + credits for the same overlapping popular titles (~300 TMDb calls per user for movies, ~450 for series). `updatePoolEnrichmentBatch` now writes them back, UPDATE-only so a personalized candidate cannot gain a pool row, and `RawCandidate.isEnriched` carries the flag out so `enrichMissingData` skips a row a previous run paid for. A candidate with no cast is **not** marked enriched — caching an empty lookup would freeze a blank card for everyone.
- **O(n²) normalisation.** `calculatePopularityScore` re-mapped and re-scanned the whole candidate array per candidate, and spread it into `Math.max`. Min/max now measured once.
- **Racy pool upsert.** SELECT-then-UPDATE-or-INSERT per candidate (~1,200 round trips per media type) replaced by chunked `ON CONFLICT`, with `sources` unioned in SQL. Candidates are deduped per batch first — ON CONFLICT cannot touch one row twice in a statement.
- **Franchise nudge was movie-blind.** The scorer identified candidates with `detectFranchiseFromTitle` (canonical names: "Marvel Cinematic Universe") while `detectMovieFranchises` keys stored preferences on `movies.collection_name` (TMDb names: "The Avengers Collection"). Two vocabularies, so the lookup missed on every enriched library and the nudge silently never fired for movies. Series were unaffected — `detectSeriesFranchises` uses the detector on both sides. `franchiseKeys` now expands both sides the same way (raw, Collection-stripped, and detector-canonical); pure, exported, pinned.
- **Recency cliff.** `1 - age/10` clamped to zero made every title ten years or older score exactly 0, and an unknown year returned 0.5 — so missing metadata outranked every real film older than five years. Replaced with exponential decay (12-year half-life), unknown year scored as old, future dates clamped.
- **Dead columns and assignments.** `discovery_candidates.pool_id` and `is_personalized` (migration 0098) were never written; both are now, `is_personalized` from `PERSONALIZED_SOURCES`. `scorer.ts` assigned `rank` through a cast onto a type with no such field, which nothing read — `storeDiscoveryCandidates` numbers rows from its own loop index.
- **Dead exports.** `getPoolCandidateByTmdbId`, `getUnenrichedPoolCandidates`, the single-row `updatePoolCandidateEnrichment`, `isInLibrary`, `hasWatched` and `fetchAllCandidates` deleted. `hasDiscoverySources` returned a hardcoded `true` under a comment saying it should check; it now reads the TMDb key, and `GET /api/discovery/prerequisites` calls it instead of re-deriving readiness inline.
- **Declined-forever.** `getRequestedTmdbIds` excludes any request not in a terminal state, but `discovery_requests.status` only ever advanced when someone loaded that one request's status endpoint. A request Seerr declined stayed `'submitted'` and suppressed its title from every future run, with nothing on screen explaining why. New `reconcile-discovery-requests` job (`discover/reconcile.ts`, daily 04:30, ahead of the 06:00 suggestions run so a decline is reflected the same night). `resolveRequestStatus` is pure and pinned: media availability outranks request state, and a still-pending request never walks backwards. Requests stranded with no `seerr_request_id` for 24h are written off as `'failed'` so the title returns. A Seerr outage leaves rows untouched.
- **Divergent quality floors.** `fetchFilteredCandidates` (behind `/expand`) hardcoded `minVoteCount: 20` against the config's 50, so expanded rows were drawn from a laxer pool than the list they get merged into and ranked against. Now defaults from `DEFAULT_DISCOVERY_CONFIG`.
- **Refresh concurrency.** The refresh route runs the pipeline inside the request; a double-click started two pipelines racing over the same delete/insert window. Guarded in-memory per user and media type, returning 409.

### Left alone deliberately

`DynamicFetchFilters.withOriginCountry` is still dropped by the `/expand` route — nothing sends it, and wiring a parameter no client uses is speculative surface. Expanded results are still not cached: expansion only runs when filters are active, and the cache is deliberately unfiltered-only, so caching them would need a filter-keyed cache (a feature, not a fix); on reload the auto-expand simply re-runs. Discovery had no admin configuration surface at the time of that pass; the addendum below records the card that now provides one.

### Addendum — F1 and F2 resolved, and the two features that made them solid

The two findings recorded above as "NOT fixed — needs a design decision" are now fixed. The decision taken was **embed the candidates**, on the strength of one observation that removes the cost objection the original write-up assumed: the shared pool already exists, so a candidate is embedded **once for the whole instance** and reused by every user and every run, rather than per user per run. That turns "an embedding call per scored candidate" into "an embedding call per newly-seen title", which on a steady-state pool is a handful per run.

**The candidate vector store.** `discovery_candidate_embeddings` (`0160`), keyed `(media_type, tmdb_id, model)` where `model` is `getEmbeddingInvocation().setId` — the same discipline as the library tables, so a provider, model or retrieval-mode change starts a new set rather than silently reusing vectors from a different space. Dimension-free `halfvec` with no index, following `0079`/`0086`: this table is read by key and scored in JavaScript, never by ANN, so it needs neither a fixed width nor the per-dimension family the library tables require. `text_hash` records the document the vector was built from, so a candidate embedded before enrichment is superseded once a richer document exists without re-embedding everything each run.

**The document is deliberately thinner, and that is only safe because discovery ranks.** `buildCandidateCanonicalText` mirrors `buildCanonicalText`'s section order and its literal labels ("Genres:", "Directed by", "Starring") but can only fill six of nine sections — TMDb gives no studios, no cinematographers, no keywords, and an overview rather than `plot_full`. Candidate vectors therefore sit systematically further from the taste centroid than library vectors do. That is a fatal problem for a threshold and a non-problem for an ordering: every candidate is thin in the same way, so the offset is common to all of them and cancels out of the comparison. The rule that follows is that a discovery similarity must never be thresholded against a constant derived from library-to-library distances, nor compared numerically with a recommender one.

**The embedding space was the real constraint, and it has one genuine exception to F-036.** Taste profiles are built in whichever space `isCenteringReady` reported at build time, recorded on `user_taste_profiles.embedding_space`. F-036 states the library mean is never stored or looked up at query time, because a profile built from the centred column is already centred and needs none — true for every existing reader. A freshly embedded candidate has never been through `refreshCenteredEmbeddings`, so it is the one case where the mean genuinely has to be recomputed: `getLibraryEmbeddingMean` runs `AVG(l2_normalize(embedding))` **once per run**, and `centreVector` l2-normalises before subtracting, matching the stored formula exactly (centring is a subtraction, so an unnormalised row is rotated by a different amount than a normalised one — a wrong *direction*, not a wrong magnitude). `resolveEmbeddingSpace` still refuses rather than falling back; a refusal costs the taste term for that run, not the run.

**Both terms are now normalised inside their own population.** Similarity is min-max scaled across the pool — the treatment popularity already had — measured **only over candidates that have a vector**, so titles that could not be embedded do not drag the floor down and inflate everyone else; a missing vector scores a neutral 0.5. Popularity is scaled **within each source** (`popularityScoresBySource`, pure and pinned). That is the fix for the scale mixing: within `trakt_trending` the watcher ordering survives and is rescaled onto the same 0–1 as TMDb's, and within `trakt_popular`/`trakt_recommendations` every value is identical so `normalize` returns **0.5 — "no popularity signal", not "unpopular"**.

**Why the two had to land together.** Fixing the popularity scaling alone would have made the product worse. With similarity dead, popularity carried 50% of the ranking, so putting Trakt on a real scale would have handed more influence to the term that was standing in for taste. With similarity alive the configured 45.5% is real again, and the popularity correction lands where it was always meant to.

**Two features shipped alongside, because the fixes were not operable without them.**

*Admin configuration.* All eleven `DiscoveryConfig` knobs plus the two pool bounds added during the fix pass are now stored as `discovery_config` in `system_settings` and edited at Admin → Recommendations → Discovery tuning. One JSON blob rather than a table, matching the other integration configs; no per-media-type split, deliberately, since every knob here is about how much to fetch and how hard to filter and none of the evidence for splitting exists — splitting later is additive, merging back is not. `sanitizeDiscoveryConfig` is pure, pinned, and applied on **both** read and write, so a blob written before a field existed still returns a complete config and a hand-edited row cannot reach a state the UI could not produce. It checks absent **before** coercing, which its own test caught: `Number(null)` is 0 and finite, so coercing first sails past the `Number.isFinite` guard and clamps a missing knob to its **minimum** rather than restoring its default — for `maxTotalCandidates` that is the difference between storing 1000 candidates and storing 50. The bounds ride to the client in the response rather than being hand-copied into the bundle. Every field carries a literal `id=` because `registry.test.ts` scans source for the anchors the admin search promises, and an id assembled from a data array is one it cannot see — the test caught that too.

*Asynchronous refresh.* The residue F9 left. The route now answers **202** and runs the pipeline in the background; the page polls the `discovery_runs` row it was already fetching. No new job type: the pipeline already moves that row from `running` to `completed`/`failed`, so the progress signal existed and simply was not used. `refreshInFlight` is released in the background promise's `finally`, not the handler's — releasing it when the response is sent would let a second click start a second run. The client poll is bounded at four minutes, because a process killed mid-run leaves the row at `running` with nothing to move it.

### Addendum — the first live run, and the two faults it exposed

Deployed and run against a real instance. Movies scored a full taste spread on every user; series came back flat at exactly 0.50 for at least one. Diagnosing it took five round trips and turned up two faults, chained.

**Fault 1, data.** `user_taste_clusters.embedding_model` for that user's series row read `openrouter:google/gemini-embedding-001` while the active model was `gemini-embedding-2`. `getUserTasteClusters` discards a cluster stamped with a different model and falls through to the legacy `user_preferences.{taste,series_taste}_embedding` — working as designed, and exactly what F-042 records: a model change reaches nobody until `rebuild-taste-profiles` is run. `generate-series-embeddings` reporting "0 series needing embeddings" is not a contradiction; the embeddings were current, the *cluster* was not, and that job does not touch taste profiles.

**Fault 2, code, and the reason it was silent.** The legacy fallback in `getUserTasteVectors` selected the column bare:

```
SELECT ${embeddingColumn} as embedding FROM user_preferences WHERE user_id = $1
```

annotated as `{ embedding: number[] }`. The column is a **halfvec, and pg returns a halfvec as a string** — so the fallback produced a ~40,000-character string where a 3,072-element array was expected. TypeScript cannot check a row annotation against a runtime driver, so nothing caught it. `maxTasteSimilarity` then compared `vector.length` — the *string* length — against the candidate's 3072, skipped every taste vector, and returned null for all 331 candidates. The caller reads null as "no taste signal" and assigns a flat neutral.

`getUserTasteClusters` on the line above does it correctly (`embedding::text` plus `parseEmbedding`), which is what made the two paths behave differently and the bug invisible for as long as the cluster path was taken.

**Why it hid.** The bug predates this work, but its output was never consumed: with the candidate embedding map always empty (the original F1 defect), `getUserTasteVectors`' return value could not affect a score. Repairing F1 is what made the older bug reachable. That is the general shape worth remembering — **fixing a dead path exposes every latent bug in the code that fed it**, and those bugs have never been exercised, so they are exactly the ones with no test coverage.

Three things fixed:

- The legacy read now casts `::text` and parses explicitly, mirroring `getUserTasteClusters`. A vector that fails to parse returns **none** rather than a broken one, because a malformed vector is silently skipped downstream and reads as an absent taste signal — the same failure in a new costume.
- `Math.min(...values)` / `Math.max(...values)` over the similarity map, replaced with a loop. The identical spread pattern had already been removed from `popularityRange` in this module and was reintroduced a few hundred lines later; `maxTotalCandidates` is now an admin slider reaching 5000, and spreading an array that large throws `RangeError`.
- A `Taste similarity measured` log line carrying `compared` and the **raw** min/max, before normalisation. The existing line reported only `embeddingsUsable` (the size of the candidate map) and the post-normalisation spread, which cannot distinguish *"the vectors could not be compared at all"* from *"they compared and every answer agreed"*. Those two have completely different causes and the same symptom, and separating them by hand is what cost the five round trips. The job-console warning also gained the candidate count and a named remedy.

**Instrument lesson.** A diagnostic that reports the size of an input is not a diagnostic. `embeddingsUsable: 331` was true, reassuring, and irrelevant — the failure was one step further on, in a comparison that consumed those 331 vectors and produced nothing. Log the output of the step that can fail, not the input to it.

## F-105

**Requesting without leaving Aperture: attribution, the search boundary, and the admin scope.** Built 2026-09-04 as phases 1–2 of making Seerr an admin-only backend. Sources read at Seerr v3.4.1 (`seerr-team/seerr`, formerly `fallenbagel/jellyseerr`) and the Overseerr OpenAPI spec.

### Everything Aperture submitted to Seerr was filed by the admin

`seerrRequest` sent only `X-Api-Key`. Seerr's `checkUser` middleware resolves an API-key request to **user id 1 — the original administrator** — unless the request also carries `X-API-User`, in which case that user becomes `req.user` for the whole request:

```ts
if (req.header('X-API-Key') === settings.main.apiKey) {
  let userId = 1;                                    // original administrator
  if (req.header('X-API-User')) {                    // act on that user's behalf
    userId = Number(req.header('X-API-User'));
  }
  user = await userRepository.findOne({ where: { id: userId } });
}
```

Requests partly escaped this because `POST /request` accepts a `userId` body field, which Aperture was already sending. Nothing else does. Measured across release tags:

| Endpoint | On-behalf-of via body | Available since |
|---|---|---|
| `POST /request` | `userId` | Overseerr |
| `POST /issue` | `userId` | **Seerr v3.4.0** (absent in v3.3.0 and all 2.x) |
| `POST /issue/:id/comment` | — | **never** (hardcodes `user: req.user` in v3.4.1) |

So the header is the only mechanism that attributes every endpoint, and it is the one that has been there longest. Adopted for requests now, and it is what makes phase 3 (issues) possible at all — without it every issue and every comment from every user would be authored by admin #1.

### Sending both attributions is a 403, not redundancy

`MediaRequest.request()` resolves `X-API-User` into `req.user` **before** it looks at the body:

```ts
let requestUser = user;                              // already the header's user
if (requestBody.userId && !requestUser.hasPermission([MANAGE_USERS, MANAGE_REQUESTS])) {
  throw new RequestPermissionError('You do not have permission to modify the request user.');
}
```

An ordinary viewer holds neither permission, so header + body together refuse for every non-admin — the exact population the feature is for. `createRequest` therefore drops the body field whenever `actAsUserId` is set.

### What the header actually changes, and what it does not

Reading the same function: the target user's REQUEST permission (~line 82) and their quota (~line 114) were **already** enforced under the body-field path. The one behavioural change is auto-approval, which reads the **caller**:

```ts
status: user.hasPermission(Permission.AUTO_APPROVE, ...) ? APPROVED : PENDING
```

Under the API key the caller was admin, so every request Aperture ever submitted was auto-approved regardless of the requester's own settings. With the header, caller and target are the same person and their own auto-approve setting decides. Requests from users without it now land pending — which is why phase 2 (approve/decline in-app) shipped alongside rather than after.

An unmapped user (`users.seerr_user_id` NULL) sends no header and falls back to the previous behaviour, so this cannot break anyone it fails to identify.

### A null answer cannot carry a refusal

`seerrRequest` returned `T | null` and logged the error body. Fine for a lookup, wrong for anything a user triggered: Seerr refuses with a specific actionable sentence ("Movie Quota exceeded", "You do not have permission to make 4K movie requests") and the user saw `'Failed to create request'`. Survivable while Seerr was a second window they could open; not survivable once Aperture is the only surface. `seerrCall` returns `{ ok, status, data, message }`; `seerrRequest` is now a thin reader over it for the lookups that genuinely do not care. Routes map an upstream 4xx to itself rather than 500, since a quota refusal is the user's answer, not a server fault.

### The search boundary is where a later native fallback is cheap or expensive

Search is proxied to Seerr's `GET /search` — itself a TMDb `search/multi` wrapper that decorates each result with `mediaInfo`, so one call answers both "what matches" and "is it already here". Chosen over calling TMDb directly because the annotation is free and it is a day less work; the cost is that in-app search goes down with Seerr, which the panel states as *"Content search is unavailable"* rather than rendering an empty list ("nothing matched" and "search is off" look identical in a list and mean opposite things).

What makes the later native fallback a drop-in rather than a rewrite is that **the route owns the response shape**. Seerr speaks `mediaType: 'tv'`, `posterPath`, and availability as integers 1–5; Aperture speaks `'series'` and decided strings. Had that vocabulary reached the web bundle, a second source would mean changing the page, its types and its translations. `sources/types.ts` holds the contract, `seerrSource.ts` the only implementation, and `resolveSearchSource()` picks the first available one — the same shape as the `WebSearchSource` registry in `assistant/discovery/sources/`. No merging: two sources would return the same TMDb rows, so the question is which is authoritative, not how to combine them.

Library membership is deliberately **not** taken from the search backend. It comes from Aperture's own `movies`/`series` tables via `attachLibraryMediaIds`, because the two can disagree — a library Seerr does not scan, or a scan that is behind — and when they do, ours decides whether the user can actually play the thing. Taking Seerr's word would offer a Request button for a film already on the server.

`mapSeerrSearchItem` is split into `seerrMapping.ts` importing nothing at runtime, so it can be pinned without dragging the core barrel and its database pool into the test process. Nine cases in `seerrMapping.test.ts`, verified to fail on an injected regression (reading `status` alone instead of `max(status, status4k)` — a 4K-only title would be offered for request again).

### Admin scope is a nullable user, not a second query

`getDiscoveryRequests`/`countDiscoveryRequests` take `userId: string | null`, null meaning every user. One `buildRequestFilter` feeds both, because a count and a page that describe different populations produce a "12 requests" header over a table of 9 — the same duplicated-predicate failure this repo splits into pure modules everywhere else. `scope=all` from a non-admin is **narrowed to their own rows rather than refused**: the toggle is only rendered for admins, so a non-admin arriving there is a stale tab, and their own list is the right answer for them.

Approve/decline is keyed by the **Aperture** request id, not the Seerr one — that is what the table on screen holds, and the local row has to be updated too or the list keeps showing the old state until `reconcile-discovery-requests` next runs. A row with no `seerr_request_id` answers 409 ("never submitted") rather than a 502 from a call that was never going to work.

### Left for later

The Emby half of `matchApertureProfileToSeerrUser` is still string matching. Seerr has no Emby columns — it stores Emby users in `jellyfinUserId`/`jellyfinUsername` with `userType = EMBY` (4), and the import endpoint is literally `POST /user/import-from-jellyfin` — but the matcher guards the id path with `provider === 'jellyfin'`, so on an Emby instance the one stable GUID both systems already hold is skipped and matching falls to email (frequently absent on Emby) then username. The compare is also `pid === String(u.jellyfinUserId).trim()` with no normalization, while Seerr runs every id through `normalizeJellyfinGuid` (strip dashes, lowercase, require 32 hex) — so a dashed-vs-undashed GUID misses even on Jellyfin. And the resolved id is cached in `users.seerr_user_id` and only recomputed when NULL, so a user deleted and re-imported in Seerr keeps a stale id forever. Not blocking: an unmatched user simply sends no header and behaves as before.

### Addendum — anything that SCORES a taste profile must be able to MAINTAIN it

Chasing the flat series term further turned up the underlying structural fault, and it is not a discovery bug.

The first live run after the parse fix showed every user comparing successfully (`compared` equal to `candidateCount` on all sixteen rows) but split cleanly into two populations by the raw similarity spread:

| Profile space | Users | Raw spread |
|---|---:|---|
| `centered` | 4 | ~0.23 wide (e.g. 0.427–0.661) |
| `raw` | 4 | **~0.04 wide** (e.g. 0.497–0.534) |

A perfect correlation with `user_taste_profiles.embedding_space`, no exceptions — F-036's centring claim reproduced on a population it was never measured against, and roughly a six-fold difference in usable signal. The raw group's cosines sit at −0.01 to +0.07, which is what a raw comparison between a thin candidate document and a centroid built from full library documents looks like.

The reason four users were still `raw` was NOT that centring was unready (`uncentred = 0` on every set, both media types) and NOT that their profiles were locked (`is_locked = false` everywhere). It was `auto_updated_at`: the centred profiles were stamped the night of the rebuild, the raw ones were from **weeks earlier**. `rebuild-taste-profiles` had never processed them.

**The gate.** `rebuildAllTasteProfiles` selected:

```
WHERE is_enabled = true AND provider_disabled = false
  AND (movies_enabled = true OR series_enabled = true)
```

while `getDiscoveryEnabledUsers` selects `is_enabled = true AND discover_enabled = true`. Those are different populations. A viewer with `discover_enabled = true` and both recommendation flags false is **scored by Discover on a profile no job will ever refresh** — through model changes, through space changes, indefinitely. On the live instance exactly four users matched that signature (aggelos, dimmous, jelena, Turamarth), and they were exactly the four narrow-spread rows in the log. The arithmetic closed too: 6 maintained + 4 unmaintained = the 10 users the discovery job reported.

Fixed in two halves, and both are required:

- the WHERE clause gains `OR discover_enabled = true`;
- the per-user `mediaTypes` list gains `|| user.discover_enabled` on both branches. That list was built purely from the two recommendation flags, so widening the gate alone would have admitted these users and then rebuilt **nothing** — an empty `mediaTypes` array, reported as success. Discovery's pipeline loops both media types unconditionally for every enabled viewer, so a discover-only viewer needs both profiles.

**The general rule, which outlives this feature.** A per-feature enablement flag creates a population, and every job that reads a shared artefact for that population has to admit it. The taste profile is read by the recommender *and* by discovery, but only the recommender's flags gated its maintenance — so adding a consumer silently created a set of users whose data rots. Any future surface that reads `user_taste_profiles` or `user_taste_clusters` has to be added to this gate at the same time, or it inherits the same fault.

**Why it stayed invisible.** The same reason as everything else in this entry: while the similarity term was pinned at 0.5, a stale profile and a fresh one produced identical output. Three independent faults — the empty candidate map, the unparsed legacy vector, and this gate — all had exactly one symptom, and each had to be removed before the next became visible. Repairing a dead path does not surface one bug; it surfaces the whole stack of them, in order.

**Instrument note.** The spread figures are what made this findable at all, and only because they were logged per user rather than aggregated. An instance-wide average would have read as a mediocre-but-plausible middle and hidden a clean bimodal split — the same trap the repo-wide "measure per user before reading an aggregate as a mechanism" invariant already warns about, encountered from a new direction.

**Still open, deliberately.** `getDiscoveryEnabledUsers` does not check `provider_disabled`, so a viewer removed from the media server still gets discovery runs. Inert on the instance measured (nobody is provider-disabled) and not changed here, since it alters who receives a feature rather than fixing a fault.

### Addendum, same day: the Emby matcher, fixed

`POST /user/import-from-jellyfin` at v3.4.1 settles what Seerr actually stores:

```ts
const newUser = new User({
  jellyfinUsername: jellyfinUser?.Name,
  jellyfinUserId:   jellyfinUser?.Id,      // stored RAW; the lookup above normalizes
  email:            jellyfinUser?.Name,    // the USERNAME, not an email address
  userType: mediaServerType === JELLYFIN ? UserType.JELLYFIN : UserType.EMBY,
});
```

So `username` is never set for an imported user, and `email` holds a username. Tracing an Emby account through the old matcher: the email tier compared Aperture's real address against Seerr's `"haris"` and missed; the id tier was skipped by `provider === 'jellyfin'`; the username tier matched `jellyfinUsername` and returned. It worked — by string comparison, on the third try, having skipped the only field that is an identifier. That is why this was invisible: the common case passes.

It fails on a rename on either side (`sync-users` sets `username` on INSERT only, and Seerr stamps `jellyfinUsername` at import), on any name needing Unicode normalization (`.toLowerCase()` alone — the lesson `helpers/titleMatch.ts` already exists for), and permanently, since `ensureSeerrUserIdForRequest` recomputes only when the column is NULL.

Rewritten as ordered tiers — **mediaServerId, email, username, displayName** — where the first tier producing **exactly one** candidate wins. Two rules carry it. A tier matching several users **stops the search** instead of falling through: a weaker signal breaking a tie the stronger one could not is noise, and the result is cached forever. And the email tier requires an `@` on **both** sides, because matching a real address against Seerr's username-in-the-email-column is a coincidence, not an identity. `normalizeMediaServerId` copies Seerr's own rule (strip dashes, lowercase, require 32 hex; anything else returns null and skips the tier).

Two failures that only the header made reachable, both now handled at the write and use sites rather than in the pure matcher:

- **A contested match was a 500.** `0104`'s `idx_users_seerr_user_id_unique` is a unique partial index and the write was a bare `UPDATE` with no catch, so a second Aperture user resolving to the same Seerr account died on an unhandled 23505. A 23505 now means at least one of the two matches is wrong, so the header is not sent — attribution is lost, which is the old behaviour, rather than a request filed under someone else's name.
- **A stale id now fails harder than it used to.** Delete and re-import someone in Seerr and they return with a new id; `checkUser` then resolves `X-API-User` to nothing, `req.user` is never set, and every route answers `isAuthenticated`'s flat 403 *"You do not have permission to access this endpoint"* — true of nobody, and (since this entry's error propagation) shown to the user verbatim. Guessing is unacceptable in both directions: leaving it strands them forever, and retrying blindly would escalate a viewer who genuinely lacks REQUEST into an admin-attributed, auto-approved request. So a 403 while acting as someone asks `GET /user/:id` which case it is. **Only a 404 clears the link** — an outage must not be read as "this account is gone" and wipe a correct mapping.

`userMapping.test.ts` is the first test in `packages/core/src/seerr/`; verified to fail on the reinstated `provider === 'jellyfin'` guard, which breaks the three Emby cases and leaves the Jellyfin one passing — the exact shape of the original bug.

### Addendum — discovery was the only per-user loop not gating on provider_disabled

Recorded above as "still open, deliberately". Closed, because the codebase had already decided and discovery was simply the outlier.

Every other per-user work loop gates on `provider_disabled = false`: both recommender pipelines (four call sites), both STRM writers, both watch-history syncs, `twinAffinity`, and `rebuildAllTasteProfiles`. `strm/cleanup.ts` goes further and treats the flag as grounds to DELETE a user's generated output. Only `getDiscoveryEnabledUsers` ignored it, so discovery kept building suggestions, spending TMDb and embedding calls, and holding Seerr request rights for viewers the media server no longer has. The column is synced from the server's own `Policy.IsDisabled` by `users/sync.ts`, so it is the media server's answer rather than an Aperture preference.

Guarded in **two** places, and the second is the one that matters. The auth plugin gates on `is_enabled` **alone** — it never reads `provider_disabled` (zero occurrences in `plugins/auth.ts`) — so a dropped viewer can still hold a valid session and press Refresh. Gating only the scheduled job would have left the manual path as the way around it, which is the same class of mistake as the enablement-flag mismatch above: two entry points to one feature, gated differently.

Deliberately NOT extended to the read routes. `GET /api/discovery/movies|series` still serves whatever candidates already exist for such a user, which is right — the guard is about spending money and holding request rights on their behalf, not about hiding rows already paid for.

### Addendum — post-fix confirmation, measured

All four faults deployed and re-measured on the same instance, same pools (candidate counts within one or two of the previous run), one `rebuild-taste-profiles` in between. The four viewers the maintenance gate had stranded:

| Viewer | Before, movie / series | After, movie / series |
|---|---|---|
| `4df29461` | 0.037 / 0.032 | **0.210 / 0.162** |
| `3a7850de` | 0.038 / 0.027 | **0.253 / 0.187** |
| `517262f8` | 0.037 / 0.034 | **0.202 / 0.188** |
| `08eb8546` | 0.040 / 0.031 | **0.241 / 0.160** |

Five to seven times wider. Across all twenty rows the raw spread now runs **0.160 to 0.288 with no outliers** — the narrow population is gone, not merely reduced, which is the shape a fixed gate should produce rather than a partial improvement.

**The control holds.** Viewers already in the centred space are unchanged to four decimal places: goca's movie row reads 0.4274–0.6605 before and 0.4274–0.6605 after. So the rebuild repaired the broken profiles without disturbing the sound ones, which is what distinguishes this from a change that simply moved everybody. `compared` equals `candidateCount` on every row, so the halfvec parse fix holds under the new profiles too.

**Series spreads sit consistently below movie spreads** — roughly 0.16–0.21 against 0.20–0.29, on every viewer without exception. Not a fault and not worth chasing: the series library is 986 titles against 12,589 movies, so both the centring mean and the taste centroid are built from an order of magnitude fewer items. Worth recording precisely so it is not mistaken for a series-side defect later; the correlation is with library size, not with media type.

**What this closes.** F-036's centring gain is now demonstrated in both directions on a population it was never measured against — first observed as a defect (four viewers stuck at a sixth of everyone else's signal), then corrected and re-measured. That is a stronger form of evidence than the original A/B, because the failure and the repair were both accidental rather than designed: nobody set out to test centring, and the split fell out of a gate nobody knew was there.

**Method note.** The whole chain was only diagnosable because the spread was logged **per viewer**. An instance-wide average across these twenty rows reads as an unremarkable ~0.15 both before and after, which would have hidden a clean bimodal split first and a complete repair second. This is the repo-wide "measure per user before reading an aggregate as a mechanism" invariant, encountered twice in one investigation from opposite directions.

### Addendum: what a viewer may see and set, and where a request says it came from

Three things the deployed page made obvious that no test could.

**The request dialog was showing every viewer the server's filesystem.** `RequestSeerrOptionsDialog` renders a root-folder dropdown reading `GET /api/seerr/service/radarr|sonarr`, both of which were `requireAuth` — so anyone with `discover_request_enabled` was handed `D:\Downloads\TV` and every other configured library path, and could file into whichever one they liked. Fixed in three places at once, because any one of them alone is a half-measure: the four `service/*` routes are `requireAdmin` now; `pickRequestOverrides` drops `rootFolder`/`profileId`/`serverId`/`languageProfileId` from a non-admin body, since the dialog is UI and the body still arrives from a client; and the gate lives **inside the dialog** rather than at its six call sites, because a rule enforced by six callers is one the seventh will forget. For a viewer the dialog is a pass-through — it confirms with `{}` on open and renders null, so the caller's options → seasons → submit sequence is unchanged and Seerr applies that user's own defaults.

`is4k` deliberately survives the filter. It is not a path, and Seerr enforces `REQUEST_4K` against the acting user itself, so forwarding it asks a question Seerr is already equipped to refuse. The dialog's own fetch is also skipped for a viewer — with the endpoints now admin-only, leaving it would fire a 403 per open that nothing renders and only the log remembers.

**A mixed relevance list is hard to read.** Seerr's own UI separates films from shows and it is right to: relevance order interleaves them, so finding "the series called X" means reading every card. The panel now renders Movies and TV shows as separate sections with counts, preserving the backend's ordering inside each. Purely presentational — one request, one response, `results` partitioned client-side.

**Every request claimed to come from Discovery.** `source` defaulted to `'discovery'` for anything posting to `/api/seerr/request`, so a title someone searched for and asked for by hand was recorded as a suggestion the recommender made — which destroys the one distinction that column exists for. `'direct'` is now a third value. `source` is an unconstrained TEXT column (`0106`), so `0161` is a comment-only migration: the vocabulary had no home in the schema, and a value nobody can discover is one the next reader duplicates under a different name.

`resolveRequestSource` is what the route trusts, not the body. **`gap_analysis` is deliberately unreachable from a browser** — those rows are written by the gap-analysis job through core, and letting a client claim that origin would corrupt the column in the other direction. Anything unrecognised reads as `discovery`, the pre-existing default and the safe fallback. Both decisions are pure and pinned in `requestOptions.test.ts`, verified to fail on an injected regression (a viewer keeping every override).

Related surface deliberately left alone: requests from `PersonDetail` still record as `discovery`. They are arguably direct too, but that is a separate call site and a separate judgement about what the column should say.

## F-106

### One column, several units — the second pass over Discover

[F-104](#f-104) repaired the taste term and closed four chained faults. Re-reading the feature afterwards turned up five more, and three of them are the same defect wearing different clothes: **a single column carrying several different quantities, with the unit recorded nowhere.**

`discovery_pool.popularity` holds TMDb's unbounded metric for the TMDb sources, a Trakt watcher count for `trakt_trending`, and a hardcoded 0 for `trakt_popular`. `RawCandidate.voteAverage` holds a rating, a genuine zero, and "no data". F-104's per-source popularity fix treated the ambiguity at the last possible moment — in the scorer — while the same ambiguity was still deciding which rows exist at all and which fields survive a merge.

#### The merge threw away everything the pool knew

`mergeWithPool` gave personalized candidates precedence by pushing the whole object and skipping the pool row. A `trakt_recommendations` candidate is constructed with `voteAverage: 0`, `voteCount: 0`, no poster and no genres, because Trakt's list payload carries none of it. So when Trakt recommended a title the pool already held with a real TMDb rating, poster, genre list and cached cast, that row was discarded and the bare Trakt row won.

The source this hits is the **highest-scoring one in the system** — `trakt_recommendations` carries `sourceScore` 1.0, the one the code itself calls "most personalized". It was reliably producing cards with a missing rating, and below `maxEnrichedCandidates` a missing poster too. It also lost `isEnriched` and `poolId`, so the run re-paid two or three TMDb requests per title the pool had already bought.

The earlier ratings work does not reach this. `NULLIF` fixed what the pool **stores** and `enrichBasicData`'s selection fixed what the pool **enriches**; both are correct, and this path never touched the pool row on its way past. **A fix applied at one end of a pipeline does not travel to the other end**, and the merge is a third place the same question gets answered.

`updatePoolEnrichmentBatch` was the other half: it wrote back cast, directors, runtime, tagline and `imdb_id` while dropping the vote average, vote count, poster, backdrop, overview, language and genre list from the same TMDb response. So an enriched pool row kept its blank rating forever and the gap could not heal itself — every viewer in every future run paid the same lookup while the pool never learned the answer.

#### The unit and the label were maintained independently

`popularityScoresBySource` normalises within the group named by `source`, which for a pool row is `sources[1]`. That array was maintained by

```sql
sources = ARRAY(SELECT DISTINCT unnest(sources || EXCLUDED.sources))
```

and `SELECT DISTINCT` with no `ORDER BY` guarantees nothing about order, while `popularity` was separately `COALESCE`'d from whichever source last supplied a non-null figure. Two independent maintainers, one implied invariant, no enforcement.

Measured on the live pool — and the same source set appears in **both orders**, which is the non-determinism showing up directly in the data:

| `sources` | rows | popularity |
|---|---:|---|
| `{tmdb_discover}` | 222 | 28.01 – 901.11 |
| `{tmdb_discover,trakt_trending}` | 38 | 28.71 – 104.79 |
| `{trakt_trending,tmdb_discover}` | 15 | 28.88 – 33.01 |
| `{tmdb_discover,trakt_popular}` | 3 | 28.02 – 54.76 |
| `{trakt_popular,tmdb_discover}` | 1 | 53.60 |

Every figure there is on TMDb's decimal scale: TMDb Discover is first in `fetchGlobalCandidates`' concatenation and the dedupe keeps the first occurrence, so a title both sources return enters as the TMDb candidate. But **16 of 279 movies list a Trakt source first**, so the scorer filed a TMDb-scaled number in a group of 15 spanning 4.13 points and min-max stretched it across the full 0–1. A title at popularity 33.01 scored **1.00** there against **0.006** in the 873-point group it belongs to. Popularity carries 27% of the blend, so that is most of a term handed out by an arbitrary array order — and **F-104's per-source fix is what created the exposure**, since before it every pool row normalised together on the one scale it actually shares.

Migration `0162` adds `popularity_source`, written in the **same expression** as `popularity` and NULL whenever the figure is, so a source is never claimed for a number that was not stored; on conflict it moves only when the number moves. The backfill is provable rather than inferred: every row in the pool lists `tmdb_discover` among its sources (there is not one Trakt-only row) and TMDb Discover is the only global source supplying a non-null popularity at all. Anything else keeps NULL, which the scorer groups separately rather than guessing at.

Ordering the `DISTINCT` would only have made the wrong answer deterministic. `sources` is now **appended to rather than rebuilt**, preserving first-seen order — which matters on its own account, because `sources[1]` still decides `calculateSourceScore`, so a title's source score could previously change between runs without the title changing.

**The same column also decided who survived the cap.** `getPoolCandidates` truncated with `ORDER BY popularity DESC NULLS LAST LIMIT maxPoolCandidates`, so once a pool outgrew the cap the `LIMIT` stopped being a bound and became a filter: every Trakt-only row went first (NULL popularity, `NULLS LAST`), then the least globally popular. That second part is the one worth naming — **this is a cap for a list scored in memory per viewer, a resource bound and not a quality judgement**, and ranking is what the scorer exists to do afterwards. Truncating by global popularity removes the niche title that best matches one person's taste *before it can ever be scored*, on the feature whose whole purpose is personalization, with similarity carrying more of the blend than popularity does.

Now `ORDER BY updated_at DESC, tmdb_id ASC` — how recently a source last offered the title, a unit every row shares. It is not an ideal answer: a whole night's batch shares a timestamp, so within a batch it decides nothing. It is an honest one, and it cannot fragment the way a mixed unit does. The `tmdb_id` tiebreak makes truncation **deterministic**, since without it Postgres may order ties freely and which rows survive would vary between runs — the same defect this entry removed from `sources`.

Measured 279 movies and 263 series against a cap of 3,000 with **zero** NULL popularity, so this could not fire on the instance where it was found, and it was changed anyway. The trigger is an admin raising `poolMaxAgeDays` (the slider reaches 365 against a default of 30), and by the time a pool has grown nobody remembers the column holds three units. **A latent trap is cheapest to fix while the reasoning that found it is still in front of you.**

#### The fallback nobody maintained

`getUserTasteVectors` fell back to `user_preferences.taste_embedding` whenever `getUserTasteClusters` came back empty. Three things are true of that column and not of the clusters:

1. **`rebuild-taste-profiles` never writes it.** Nothing in `taste-profile/` mentions the column — it is written only by the two *recommender* pipelines. So for the discover-only viewers F-104's gate fix just admitted, it is still unmaintained: the same fault, one artefact over.
2. **It carries no `embedding_model`.** `getUserTasteClusters` discards a stale model explicitly; this read had no way to.
3. **It carries no `embedding_space`.** That column exists on `user_taste_profiles` alone (`0154`), so `getProfileEmbeddingSpace` asked one table which space to use and the answer was applied to a vector from another table maintained by another job.

Together those let the fallback return a vector from a superseded model, in an unknown space, labelled `centered` by a row describing something else — and the candidates were then centred to match. `maxTasteSimilarity`'s width guard catches a dimension change, but a **same-width** model change (gemini-embedding-001 → -2, both 3072) passes it and yields a confident cosine between two unrelated spaces. That is exactly what `resolveEmbeddingSpace` exists to refuse, **reached by a path that went around it**.

Deleted rather than repaired. Gating it on a model and a space it does not record means adding both, to a path that should be empty. No clusters at the active model now means no taste term and a neutral 0.5 — the state the feature was in before F-104 — plus a warn naming `rebuild-taste-profiles`. The halfvec-as-string parse F-104 records went with it: a real fix to a real bug, which existed only to make a fallback work that should not run.

#### The diagnostic that shipped was not the one that worked

F-104's own instrument note says the chain was only diagnosable because the spread was logged per viewer. The line that shipped to the **job console** read `candidatesToStore[].similarityScore` — the value *after* min-max normalisation across the pool — so its minimum is 0 and its maximum is 1 by construction whenever any two candidates differ. The first live run printed exactly that:

```
🎯 goca: taste match 0.00–1.00 across 245 movies
```

It could distinguish "completely flat" from "not flat", and nothing else. The 0.037-wide band that started the whole investigation would have rendered identically. The raw figures existed only inside the scorer, and went to the container log — which the code's own comment says is not where an operator looks after a deploy.

`scoreCandidates` now returns `{ candidates, taste }`, the shape the recommender's own `scoreCandidates` already uses for `{ candidates, weights }`, and the console reports raw min/max/spread plus **`compared` against the candidate count** — the pair that separates "could not compare at all" from "compared and agreed", which cost five round trips to tell apart by hand.

**The general rule: a normalised measure cannot report on its own normalisation.** Min-max output is a rank, and a rank always spans its full range; if the instrument is meant to catch a collapsed input, it has to read the input.

#### Once per run means once per job

`runDiscoveryForUser` is called `users × 2` times, and each call re-asked the library for two constants: `AVG(l2_normalize(embedding::vector))` over 12,589 × 3,072 halfvec — a ~77 MB read cast to ~155 MB of float4 and 38.6M additions — and a `COUNT(*) FILTER` over the same table. Twenty full-table aggregations a night for one vector. The module's own comment already claimed the mean was computed "once per run, not once per candidate": true, and **the run it meant was the wrong one**. Both are now cached per (media type, set id) and cleared explicitly at the top of a run rather than given a TTL, so the lifetime is a fact about the code and not a guess about the clock — a stale mean is a wrong *direction*, not a wrong scale (F-036 rule 6).

#### The percentage was a rank, so now it says so

`discovery_candidates.similarity_score` stores the pool-normalised value and `DiscoveryDetailPopper` rendered it as `(similarityScore * 100).toFixed(0)`. Min-max means the batch's best candidate is 1.0 and its worst 0.0 **by construction**, so the top pick always read **100%** and the bottom **0%**, in every batch, forever.
